It comes ultimately from mathematics: the summation notation traditionally uses i for the first index, j for the second, and so on. Example (from http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Summation):

It's also used that way for collections of things, like if you have a bunch of variables x1, x2, ... xn, then an arbitrary one will be known as xi.

As for why it's that way, I imagine SLaks is correct and it's because I is the first letter in Index.

-
@SLott: And why is i used in math? –  SLaks Nov 9 '10 at 20:31
@Slaks: That's a good question. I actually think that "i" might come from integer more than index. I also think it's easy to write on a chalkboard. Back in the 70's we were cautioned to be sure that we wrote our "i" and "j" in a perfectly distinctive manner. Indeed, we were told to draw our "i" exactly like a backwards "j" to be perfectly clear about it. –  S.Lott Nov 9 '10 at 20:38
@S.Lott: I doubt it comes from integer. We use ℤ (blackboard bold Z) for the integers because it stands for the German word Zahlen, which means numbers. Assuming that the summation notation arose at around the same time, the German-based notation would be expected—and the German word for index is Index, which also begins with an i. And I can't think of many letters which are particularly hard to write on a chalkboard. Well, except ξ :-) –  Antal S-Z Nov 9 '10 at 22:52
"This convention exists because this convention has existed before." That's kind of how etymology works: nobody knows why a particular noise had a particular meaning in PIE. It would be nice to explain the mathematical convention, but the question is "why are they used as counters in control structures", so until someone produces an answer that goes back further, this is the best. It does at least speculate why it's used in mathematics. A proper mathematical historian could find out, maybe using a binary chop. Euclid didn't use it, I bet von Neumann did. How about Cauchy? He used indexes a lot –  Steve Jessop Nov 9 '10 at 23:14
I think it was Lagrange who recommended using a,b,c for constants of a function, and x,y,z for variables of a function. i,j,k denote the 'directions' of a vector, and the factors of a formal sum like this can be construed as degrees of freedom [directions, by another name] of the equation. –  David Dec 5 '10 at 19:57

I believe it dates back to Fortran. Variables starting with I through Q were integer by default, the others were real. This meant that I was the first integer variable, and J the second, etc., so they fell towards use in loops.

-
I think this comes from the way mathematicians seem to like to use i, j, k as handy subscripts for summation and matrix multiplication indices and what-not. I remember reading in an early Fortran II manual something about that. (Yes, Fortran II.) –  S.Lott Nov 9 '10 at 19:51
Yes, FORTRAN originates these conventions. –  Xepoch Nov 10 '10 at 0:26
Called implicit declaration... –  Xepoch Nov 10 '10 at 4:20
I was taught I through N not Q. Google seems to mostly agree. (different versions of FORTRAN?) –  Hugh Allen Nov 12 '10 at 14:14
@learnvst - because mathematicians had been using i,j for at least a century beforehand. –  Martin Beckett Nov 18 '10 at 17:49

Mathematicians were using i,j,k to designate integers in algebra (subscripts, series, summations etc) long before (e.g 1836 or 1816) computers were around (this is the origin of the FORTRAN variable type defaults). The habit of using letters from the end of the alphabet (...,x,y,z) for unknown variables and from the beginning (a,b,c...) for constants is generally attributed to Rene Descartes, (see also here) so I assume i,j,k...n (in the middle of the alphabet) for integers is likely due to him too.

i = integer

Comes from Fortran where integer variables had to start with the letters I through N and real variables started with the other letters. Thus I was the first and shortest integer variable name. Fortran was one of the earliest programming languages in widespread use and the habits developed by programmers using it carried over to other languages.

EDIT: I have no problem with the answer that it derives from mathematics. Undoubtedly that is where the Fortran designers got their inspiration. The fact is, for me anyway, when I started to program in Fortran we used I, J, K, ... for loop counters because they were short and the first legally allowed variable names for integers. As a sophomore in H.S. I had probably heard of Descartes (and a very few others), but made very little connection to mathematics when programming. In fact, the first course I took was called "Fortran for Business" and was taught not by the math faculty, but the business/econ faculty.

For me, at least, the naming of variables had little to do with mathematics, but everything due to the habits I picked up writing Fortran code that I carried into other languages.

I'm pretty sure it was FORTRAN. –  Cade Roux Jan 17 '09 at 23:43<#sep#>-

#include <bits/stdc++.h>
using namespace std;

struct pair_hash {
    size_t operator()(const pair<int, int>& p) const {
        return p.first * 10007 + p.second;
    }
};

vector<pair<int, int>> directions = {{-1, 0}, {1, 0}, {0, -1}, {0, 1}};

vector<pair<int, int>> find_path(int n, const pair<int, int>& start, const pair<int, int>& end, const unordered_set<pair<int, int>, pair_hash>& forbidden) {
    vector<pair<int, int>> path;
    if (start == end) return path;

    queue<pair<int, int>> q;
    map<pair<int, int>, pair<int, int>> parent;

    q.push(start);
    parent[start] = {-1, -1};

    while (!q.empty()) {
        auto current = q.front();
        q.pop();

        for (const auto& dir : directions) {
            int nx = current.first + dir.first;
            int ny = current.second + dir.second;
            pair<int, int> next(nx, ny);

            if (nx < 1 || nx > n || ny < 1 || ny > n)
                continue;
            if (forbidden.count(next))
                continue;
            if (parent.find(next) != parent.end())
                continue;

            parent[next] = current;
            q.push(next);

            if (next == end) {
                pair<int, int> curr = next;
                while (curr != start) {
                    path.push_back(curr);
                    curr = parent[curr];
                }
                reverse(path.begin(), path.end());
                return path;
            }
        }
    }
    return path;
}

vector<pair<int, int>> find_path_k(int n, const vector<vector<int>>& grid, const pair<int, int>& start, const pair<int, int>& end, const unordered_set<pair<int, int>, pair_hash>& forbidden) {
    vector<pair<int, int>> path;
    if (start == end) return path;

    queue<pair<int, int>> q;
    map<pair<int, int>, pair<int, int>> parent;

    q.push(start);
    parent[start] = {-1, -1};

    while (!q.empty()) {
        auto current = q.front();
        q.pop();

        for (const auto& dir : directions) {
            int nx = current.first + dir.first;
            int ny = current.second + dir.second;
            pair<int, int> next(nx, ny);

            if (nx < 1 || nx > n || ny < 1 || ny > n)
                continue;
            if (forbidden.count(next))
                continue;
            if (grid[nx][ny] != -1)
                continue;
            if (parent.find(next) != parent.end())
                continue;

            parent[next] = current;
            q.push(next);

            if (next == end) {
                pair<int, int> curr = next;
                while (curr != start) {
                    path.push_back(curr);
                    curr = parent[curr];
                }
                reverse(path.begin(), path.end());
                return path;
            }
        }
    }
    return path;
}

pair<int, int> find_temporary_position(int n, const vector<vector<int>>& grid, const pair<int, int>& start, const unordered_set<pair<int, int>, pair_hash>& forbidden) {
    queue<pair<int, int>> q;
    map<pair<int, int>, bool> visited;

    q.push(start);
    visited[start] = true;

    while (!q.empty()) {
        auto current = q.front();
        q.pop();

        if (grid[current.first][current.second] == -1 && !forbidden.count(current)) {
            return current;
        }

        for (const auto& dir : directions) {
            int nx = current.first + dir.first;
            int ny = current.second + dir.second;
            pair<int, int> next(nx, ny);

            if (nx < 1 || nx > n || ny < 1 || ny > n)
                continue;
            if (forbidden.count(next))
                continue;
            if (visited.count(next))
                continue;

            visited[next] = true;
            q.push(next);
        }
    }

    for (int x = 1; x <= n; x++) {
        for (int y = 1; y <= n; y++) {
            pair<int, int> pos(x, y);
            if (grid[x][y] == -1 && !forbidden.count(pos)) {
                return pos;
            }
        }
    }
    return {-1, -1};
}

int main() {
    ios_base::sync_with_stdio(false);
    cin.tie(nullptr);

    int n, m;
    cin >> n >> m;

    vector<pair<int, int>> initial(m), targets(m);
    for (int i = 0; i < m; i++) {
        cin >> initial[i].first >> initial[i].second;
    }
    for (int i = 0; i < m; i++) {
        cin >> targets[i].first >> targets[i].second;
    }

    vector<pair<int, int>> current = initial;
    vector<vector<int>> grid(n + 1, vector<int>(n + 1, -1));
    for (int i = 0; i < m; i++) {
        int x = current[i].first;
        int y = current[i].second;
        grid[x][y] = i;
    }

    vector<tuple<int, int, int, int>> moves;

    for (int i = m - 1; i >= 0; i--) {
        auto& start = current[i];
        const auto& end = targets[i];
        if (start == end) continue;

        unordered_set<pair<int, int>, pair_hash> forbidden;
        for (int j = i + 1; j < m; j++) {
            forbidden.insert(targets[j]);
        }

        vector<pair<int, int>> path = find_path(n, start, end, forbidden);

        for (auto& step : path) {
            int nx = step.first, ny = step.second;
            if (grid[nx][ny] == -1) {
                moves.emplace_back(start.first, start.second, nx, ny);
                grid[start.first][start.second] = -1;
                grid[nx][ny] = i;
                start = {nx, ny};
            } else {
                int k = grid[nx][ny];
                unordered_set<pair<int, int>, pair_hash> forbidden_k;
                for (int j = i; j < m; j++) {
                    forbidden_k.insert(targets[j]);
                }
                pair<int, int> temp_pos = find_temporary_position(n, grid, current[k], forbidden_k);
                vector<pair<int, int>> k_path = find_path_k(n, grid, current[k], temp_pos, forbidden_k);

                for (auto& k_step : k_path) {
                    int kx1 = current[k].first, ky1 = current[k].second;
                    int kx2 = k_step.first, ky2 = k_step.second;
                    moves.emplace_back(kx1, ky1, kx2, ky2);
                    grid[kx1][ky1] = -1;
                    grid[kx2][ky2] = k;
                    current[k] = {kx2, ky2};
                }

                moves.emplace_back(start.first, start.second, nx, ny);
                grid[start.first][start.second] = -1;
                grid[nx][ny] = i;
                start = {nx, ny};
            }
        }
    }

    cout << moves.size() << '\n';
    for (const auto& move : moves) {
        int x1, y1, x2, y2;
        tie(x1, y1, x2, y2) = move;
        cout << x1 << ' ' << y1 << ' ' << x2 << ' ' << y2 << '\n';
    }

    return 0;
}<#sep#>8 \approx 67.0153496087547
   \]

4. **Compare the Results:**

   Compare the two results:
   \[
   97 \ln(2) \approx 67.23527651431469
   \]
   \[
   61 \ln(3) \approx 67.0153496087547
   \]

   Since \(67.0153496087547 < 67.23527651431469\), it follows that:
   \[
   \ln(3^{61}) < \ln(2^{97})
   \]

5. **Conclusion:**

   Because the natural logarithm function is monotonically increasing, the inequality of the logarithms implies the inequality of the original numbers:
   \[
   3^{61} < 2^{97}
   \]

Therefore, the minimum of \(2^{97}\) and \(3^{61}\) is:
\[
\boxed{3^{61}}

These symbols were used as matrix indexes in mathematics long before electronic computers were invented.

-

I think it's most likely derived from index (in the mathematical sense) - it's used commonly as an index in sums or other set-based operations, and most likely has been used that way since before there were programming languages.

-
Yup. The mathematic notation for a sum like Y = Σ Xi precedes every programming language. –  Treb Mar 23 '09 at 0:30

There's a preference in maths for using consecutive letters in the alphabet for "anonymous" variables used in a similar way. Hence, not just "i, j, k", but also "f, g, h", "p, q, r", "x, y, z" (rarely with "u, v, w" prepended), and "α, β, γ".

Now "f, g, h" and "x, y, z" are not used freely: the former is for functions, the latter for dimensions. "p, q, r" are also often used for functions.

Then there are other constraints on available sequences: "l" and "o" are avoided, because they look too much like "1" and "0" in many fonts. "t" is often used for time, "d & δ" for differentials, and "a, s, m, v" for the physical measures of acceleration, displacement, mass, and velocity. That leaves not so many gaps of three consecutive letters without unwanted associations in mathematics for indices.

Then, as several others have noticed, conventions from mathematics had a strong influence on early programming conventions, and "α, β, γ" weren't available in many early character sets.

-
Agreed. Although, a, b, c is a fairly commonly used sequence despite a having multiple meanings... –  Stobor Nov 17 '10 at 5:25

I found another possible answer that could be that i, j, and k come from Hamilton's Quaternions.

Euler picked i for the imaginary unit.

Hamilton needed two more square roots of -1: ii = jj = kk = ijk = -1

Hamilton was really influential, and quaternions were the standard way to do 3D analysis before 1900. By then, mathematicians were used to thinking of (ijk) as a matched set. Vector calculus replaced quaternionic analysis in the 1890s because it was a better way to write Maxwell's equations. But people tended to write vector quantities as like this: (3i-2j+k) instead of (3,-2,1). So (ijk) became the standard basis vectors in R^3.

Finally, physicists started using group theory to describe symmetries in systems of differential equations. So (ijk) started to connote "vectors that get swapped around by permutation groups," then drifted towards "index-like things that take on all possible values in some specified set," which is basically what they mean in a for loop.

at last a different approach to the question, interesting point +1 –  Fabian Dec 5 '10 at 17:20

a seems an array
b seems another array
c seems a language name
d seems another language name
e seems exception
f looks bad in combination with "for" (for f, a pickup?)
g seems g force
h seems height


i seems an index
j seems i (another index)


k seems a constant k
l seems a number one (1)
m seems a matrix
n seems a node
o seems an output
p sounds like a pointer
q seems a queue
r seems a return value
s seems a string
t looks like time
u reserved for UVW mapping or electic phase
v reserved for UVW mapping or electic phase or a vector
w reserved for UVW mapping or electic phase or a weight
x seems an axis (or an unknown variable)
y seems an axis
z seems a third axis

One sunny afternoon, Archimedes what pondering (as was usual for sunny afternoons) and ran into his buddy Eratosthenes.

Archimedes said, "Archimedes to Eratosthenes greeting! I'm trying to come up with a solution to the ratio of several spherical rigid bodies in equilibrium. I wish to iterate over these bodies multiple times, but I'm having a frightful time keeping track of how many iterations I've done!"

Eratosthenes said, "Why Archimedes, you ripe plum of a kidder, you could merely mark successive rows of lines in the sand, each keeping track of the number of iterations you've done within iteration!"

Archimedes cried out to the world that his great friend was undeniably a shining beacon of intelligence for coming up with such a simple solution. But Archimedes remarked that he likes to walk in circles around his sand pit while he ponders. Thus, there was risk of losing track of which row was on top, and which was on bottom.

"Perhaps I should mark these rows with a letter of the alphabet just off to the side so that I will always know which row is which! What think you of that?" he asked, then added, "But Eratosthenes... whatever letters shall I use?"

Eratosthenes was sure he didn't know which letters would be best, and said as much to Archimedes. But Archimedes was unsatisfied and continued to prod the poor librarian to choose, at least, the two letters that he would require for his current sphere equilibrium solution.

Eratosthenes, finally tired of the incessant request for two letters, yelled, "I JUST DON'T KNOW!!!"

So Archimedes chose the first two letters in Eratosthenes' exclamatory sentence, and thanked his friend for the contribution.

These symbols were quickly adopted by ancient Greek Java developers, and the rest is, well... history.

#include <bits/stdc++.h>
using namespace std;

typedef long long ll;
const ll INF = 1e18;

struct Edge {
    int to;
    ll weight;
    Edge(int t, ll w) : to(t), weight(w) {}
};

struct City {
    int id;
    ll d;
    City(int id, ll d) : id(id), d(d) {}
    bool operator<(const City& other) const { return d < other.d; }
};

vector<ll> dijkstra(int start, int n, const vector<vector<Edge>>& adj) {
    vector<ll> dist(n, INF);
    priority_queue<pair<ll, int>, vector<pair<ll, int>>, greater<>> pq;
    dist[start] = 0;
    pq.emplace(0, start);
    while (!pq.empty()) {
        auto [d, u] = pq.top();
        pq.pop();
        if (d > dist[u]) continue;
        for (const Edge& e : adj[u]) {
            ll new_dist = d + e.weight;
            if (new_dist < dist[e.to]) {
                dist[e.to] = new_dist;
                pq.emplace(new_dist, e.to);
            }
        }
    }
    return dist;
}

int main() {
    ios_base::sync_with_stdio(false);
    cin.tie(nullptr);

    int n, m;
    cin >> n >> m;
    int s, t;
    cin >> s >> t;
    s--; t--;
    vector<ll> p(n);
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) cin >> p[i];
    vector<vector<Edge>> adj(n);
    for (int i = 0; i < m; i++) {
        int u, v, w;
        cin >> u >> v >> w;
        u--; v--;
        adj[u].emplace_back(v, w);
        adj[v].emplace_back(u, w);
    }

    vector<ll> d_s = dijkstra(s, n, adj);
    vector<ll> d_t = dijkstra(t, n, adj);

    vector<City> tavas, nafas;
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        tavas.emplace_back(i, d_s[i]);
        nafas.emplace_back(i, d_t[i]);
    }
    sort(tavas.begin(), tavas.end());
    sort(nafas.begin(), nafas.end());

    vector<bool> taken(n, false);
    ll tavas_score = 0, nafas_score = 0;
    bool is_tavas_turn = true;

    while (true) {
        ll sum = 0;
        int cnt = 0;
        if (is_tavas_turn) {
            for (const City& c : tavas) {
                if (!taken[c.id]) {
                    sum += p[c.id];
                    taken[c.id] = true;
                    cnt++;
                }
            }
            tavas_score += sum;
        } else {
            for (const City& c : nafas) {
                if (!taken[c.id]) {
                    sum += p[c.id];
                    taken[c.id] = true;
                    cnt++;
                }
            }
            nafas_score += sum;
        }
        if (cnt == 0) break;
        is_tavas_turn = !is_tavas_turn;
    }

    if (tavas_score > nafas_score) cout << "Break a heart\n";
    else if (tavas_score < nafas_score) cout << "Cry\n";
    else cout << "Flowers\n";
}<#sep#>Find all functions \( f: \mathbb{R} \rightarrow \mathbb{R} \) satisfying the equation
\[ f(f(x + f(y))) + f(x + y) = x + f(f(y)) + f(f(y + f(x))) \]
for all real numbers \( x \) and \( y \).

<think>
Okay, let's try to tackle this functional equation problem. The equation given is:

\[ f(f(x + f(y))) + f(x + y) = x + f(f(y)) + f(f(y + f(x))) \]

Hmm, functional equations can be tricky, but usually, you can find solutions by making educated guesses or by substituting specific values for x and y to simplify the equation. Let me start by plugging in some common values, like x = 0 or y = 0, to see if that helps.

First, let's try x = 0. Then the equation becomes:

\[ f(f(0 + f(y))) + f(0 + y) = 0 + f(f(y)) + f(f(y + f(0))) \]

Simplifying:

\[ f(f(f(y))) + f(y) = f(f(y)) + f(f(y + f(0))) \]

Hmm, that's interesting. Let's denote c = f(0) to make it simpler. So:

\[ f(f(f(y))) + f(y) = f(f(y)) + f(f(y + c)) \]

Not sure what to do with this yet. Maybe subtract f(f(y)) from both sides:

\[ f(f(f(y))) = f(f(y + c)) \]

So, if we let z = f(y), then:

\[ f(z) = f(f(y + c)) \]

Wait, but z is f(y), so maybe that's not directly helpful. Alternatively, if f is injective (one-to-one), then we could say that f(f(y)) = f(y + c), but we don't know if f is injective yet. Let's keep that in mind.

Next, maybe try y = 0. Let's substitute y = 0 into the original equation:

\[ f(f(x + f(0))) + f(x + 0) = x + f(f(0)) + f(f(0 + f(x))) \]

Simplifying:

\[ f(f(x + c)) + f(x) = x + f(c) + f(f(f(x))) \]

Wait, from earlier, when we set x = 0, we had f(f(f(y))) = f(f(y + c)). If we apply this to the term f(f(f(x))) here, replacing y with x, we get:

f(f(f(x))) = f(f(x + c))

So substituting that into the equation from y = 0:

\[ f(f(x + c)) + f(x) = x + f(c) + f(f(x + c)) \]

Now, subtract f(f(x + c)) from both sides:

\[ f(x) = x + f(c) \]

Whoa! So that simplifies to f(x) = x + f(c). Let's denote d = f(c), so f(x) = x + d. So the function is linear, which is a good sign. Now, we can check if this works and find d.

But wait, let's make sure. If f(x) = x + d, then let's substitute back into the original equation to verify and find d.

First, compute each term:

Left-hand side (LHS):

f(f(x + f(y))) + f(x + y)

Since f(z) = z + d, then f(y) = y + d, so f(x + f(y)) = f(x + y + d) = (x + y + d) + d = x + y + 2d.

Then, f(f(x + f(y))) = f(x + y + 2d) = (x + y + 2d) + d = x + y + 3d.

Next, f(x + y) = (x + y) + d.

So LHS total is (x + y + 3d) + (x + y + d) = 2x + 2y + 4d.

Right-hand side (RHS):

x + f(f(y)) + f(f(y + f(x)))

Compute f(f(y)): f(y + d) = (y + d) + d = y + 2d.

Compute f(x) = x + d, so y + f(x) = y + x + d.

Then f(y + f(x)) = f(x + y + d) = (x + y + d) + d = x + y + 2d.

Then f(f(y + f(x))) = f(x + y + 2d) = (x + y + 2d) + d = x + y + 3d.

Thus, RHS is x + (y + 2d) + (x + y + 3d) = x + y + 2d + x + y + 3d = 2x + 2y + 5d.

So LHS is 2x + 2y + 4d, RHS is 2x + 2y + 5d. For these to be equal for all x and y, we need 4d = 5d, which implies d = 0.

So f(x) = x + d = x + 0 = x. So f(x) = x.

Now, we need to check if this function actually satisfies the original equation.

Let's test f(x) = x.

Left-hand side:

f(f(x + f(y))) + f(x + y) = f(f(x + y)) + f(x + y) = f(x + y) + (x + y) = (x + y) + (x + y) = 2x + 2y.

Right-hand side:

x + f(f(y)) + f(f(y + f(x))) = x + f(y) + f(y + x) = x + y + (y + x) = x + y + y + x = 2x + 2y.

So both sides are equal, so f(x) = x is indeed a solution.

But wait, the problem says "find all functions". So is f(x) = x the only solution? Let's see if there are other possibilities.

Earlier steps led us to f(x) = x + d, which only works when d = 0, so f(x) = x is the only linear solution. But maybe there are non-linear solutions?

Wait, when we substituted y = 0, we ended up with f(x) = x + f(c), which suggests that the function is linear, given that the equation simplifies to that. But how did we get there? Let's check again.

After substituting y = 0, we had:

f(f(x + c)) + f(x) = x + f(c) + f(f(f(x)))

Then we used the result from x = 0 substitution: f(f(f(y))) = f(f(y + c)).

So replacing y with x in that result: f(f(f(x))) = f(f(x + c)).

Therefore, substituting back into the equation from y = 0:

f(f(x + c)) + f(x) = x + f(c) + f(f(x + c))

Subtract f(f(x + c)) from both sides:

f(x) = x + f(c)

So this suggests that f(x) is linear with slope 1 and intercept d = f(c) = f(f(0)). But when we then checked the equation, we found that d must be zero. Therefore, the only solution is f(x) = x.

But let's verify again if there could be any other solutions. Suppose that f is not linear. However, the step above where substituting y = 0 gives f(x) = x + f(c) seems to force f to be linear. So if f(x) = x + d is the only possible form, and d must be zero, then f(x) = x is the only solution.

Alternatively, perhaps there's a mistake in the reasoning. Let's check again.

From y = 0 substitution:

f(f(x + c)) + f(x) = x + f(c) + f(f(f(x)))

But if we had f(f(f(x))) = f(f(x + c)) from x = 0 substitution (with y replaced by x), then the equation becomes:

f(f(x + c)) + f(x) = x + f(c) + f(f(x + c))

Therefore, f(x) = x + f(c), so f(x) = x + d, where d = f(c). Then, substituting back into the original equation gives d = 0.

Therefore, indeed, the only solution is f(x) = x.

i think it's because a lot of loops use an Int type variable to do the counting, like

for (int i = 0; etc


and when you type, you actually speak it out in your head (like when you read), so in your mind, you say 'int....'

and when you have to make up a letter right after that 'int....' , you say / type the 'i' because that is the first letter you think of when you've just said 'int'

like you spell a word to kids who start learning reading you spell words for them by using names, like this:

WORD spells William W, Ok O, Ruby R, Done D

So you say Int I, Double d, Float f, string s etc. based on the first letter.

And j is used because when you have done int I, J follows right after it.

I think it's a combination of the other mentioned reasons :

For starters, 'i' was commonly used by mathematicians in their notation, and in the early days of computing with languages that weren't binary (ie had to be parsed and lexed in some fashion), the vast majority of users of computers were also mathematicians (... and scientists and engineers) so the notation fell into use in computer languages for programming loops, and has kind of just stuck around ever since.

Combine this with the fact that screen space in those very early days was very limited, as was memory, it made sense to keep shorter variable names.

-

Possibly historical ?

FORTRAN, aurguably the first high level language, defined i,j,k,l,m as Integer datatypes by default, and loops could only be controlled by integer variable, the convention continues ?

eg:

do 100 i= j,100,5 .... 100 continue ....

-

i = iterator, i = index, i = integer

Which ever you figure "i" stands for it still "fits the bill".

Also, unless you have only a single line of code within that loop, you should probably be naming the iterator/index/integer variable to something more meaningful. Like: employeeIndex

BTW, I usually use "i" in my simple iterator loops; unless of course it contains multiple lines of code.

-

i = iota, j = jot; both small changes.

iota is the smallest letter in the greek alphabet; in the English language it's meaning is linked to small changes, as in "not one iota" (from a phrase in the New Testament: "until heaven and earth pass away, not an iota, not a dot, will pass from the Law" (Mt 5:18)).

A counter represents a small change in a value.

And from iota comes jot (iot), which is also a synonym for a small change.

-

Well from Mathematics: (for latin letters)

a,b: used as constants or as integers for a rational number
c: a constant
d: derivative
e: Euler's number
f,g,h: functions
i,j,k: are indexes (also unit vectors and the quaternions)
l: generally not used. looks like 1
m,n: are rows and columns of matrices or as integers for rational numbers
o: also not used (unless you're in little o notation)
p,q: often used as primes
r: sometimes a spatial change of variable other times related to prime numbers
s,t: spatial and temporal variables or s is used as a change of variable for t
u,v,w: change of variable
x,y,z: variables

-

Many possible main reasons, I guess:

• mathematicians use i and j for Natural Numbers in formulas (the ones that use Complex Numbers rarely, at least), so this carried over to programming
• from C, i hints to int. And if you need another int then i2 is just way too long, so you decide to use j.
• there are languages where the first letter decides the type, and i is then an integer.
-

It comes from Fortran, where i,j,k,l,m,n are implicitly integers.

-

I commonly see the variables ii, jj, kk, mm, nn etc. used as counters in DSP code. The variables i and j left on their own are too easily confused with complex number notation. Counter variables l and ll are not commonly used because they are easily confused with the digit 1.

TI-83+ Z-80 Basic. ;-) –  Tim Gostony Feb 22 '12 at 2:34

It definitely comes from mathematics, which long preceded computer programming.

So, where did if come from in math? My completely uneducated guess is that it's as one fellow said, mathematicians like to use alphabetic clusters for similar things -- f, g, h for functions; x, y, z for numeric variables; p, q, r for logical variables; u, v, w for other sets of variables, especially in calculus; a, b, c for a lot of things. i, j, k comes in handy for iterative variables, and that about exhausts the possibilities. Why not m, n? Well, they are used for integers, but more often the end points of iterations rather than the iterative variables themselves.

Someone should ask a historian of mathematics.

-

See everyone speaks the same language. No matter even if it is a programming language. :-)

-

Counters are so common in programs, and in the early days of computing, everything was at a premium...
Programmers naturally tried to conserve pixels, and the 'i' required fewer pixels than any other letter to represent. (Mathematicians, being lazy, picked it for the same reason - as the smallest glyph).
As stated previously, 'j' just naturally followed...

I use it for a number of reasons.

• Usually my loops are int based, so you make a complete triangle on the keyboard typing "int i" with the exception of the space I handle with my thumb. This is a very fast sequence to type.

• The "i" could stand for iterator, integer, increment, or index, each of which makes logical sense.

With my personal uses set aside, the theory of it being derived from FORTRAN is correct, where integer vars used letters I - N.

I learned FORTRAN on a Control Data Corp. 3100 in 1965. Variables starting with 'I' through 'N' were implied to be integers. Ex: 'IGGY' and 'NORB' were integers, 'XMAX' and 'ALPHA' were floating-point. However, you could override this through explicit declaration.

It's interesting that so many people point to summation formulas in math. Certainly i and j are common, but I would say n is as common as i (and m as j if using n instead<#sep#>-

#include <vector>
#include <algorithm>
#include <tuple>
#include <numeric>
using namespace std;

vector<pair<int, int>> factorize(int n) {
    vector<pair<int, int>> factors;
    for (int i = 2; i * i <= n; ++i) {
        if (n % i == 0) {
            int cnt = 0;
            while (n % i == 0) {
                cnt++;
                n /= i;
            }
            factors.emplace_back(i, cnt);
        }
    }
    if (n > 1) {
        factors.emplace_back(n, 1);
    }
    return factors;
}

bool is_2_type(int d, const vector<pair<int, int>>& b_factors) {
    auto d_factors = factorize(d);
    for (const auto& [p, e] : d_factors) {
        bool found = false;
        for (const auto& [bp, be] : b_factors) {
            if (p == bp) {
                found = true;
                break;
            }
        }
        if (!found) return false;
    }
    return true;
}

bool is_3_type(int d, int b) {
    return (b - 1) % d == 0;
}

bool is_11_type(int d, int b) {
    return (b + 1) % d == 0;
}

int compute_k(int d, const vector<pair<int, int>>& b_factors) {
    auto d_factors = factorize(d);
    int k = 0;
    for (const auto& [p, e] : d_factors) {
        int b_exp = 0;
        for (const auto& [bp, be] : b_factors) {
            if (p == bp) {
                b_exp = be;
                break;
            }
        }
        int k_p = (e + b_exp - 1) / b_exp; // ceil(e / b_exp)
        k = max(k, k_p);
    }
    return k;
}

vector<int> get_factors(int d) {
    vector<int> factors;
    for (int i = 1; i * i <= d; ++i) {
        if (d % i == 0) {
            if (i != 1) factors.push_back(i);
            if (i != d / i && d / i != 1) factors.push_back(d / i);
        }
    }
    sort(factors.begin(), factors.end());
    factors.erase(unique(factors.begin(), factors.end()), factors.end());
    return factors;
}

bool check_six_type(int d, int b, const vector<pair<int, int>>& b_factors) {
    vector<int> factors = get_factors(d);
    for (int f : factors) {
        int m = d / f;
        if (gcd(f, m) != 1) continue;
        bool f2 = is_2_type(f, b_factors);
        bool f3 = is_3_type(f, b);
        bool f11 = is_11_type(f, b);
        if (!f2 && !f3 && !f11) continue;
        bool m2 = is_2_type(m, b_factors);
        bool m3 = is_3_type(m, b);
        bool m11 = is_11_type(m, b);
        if ((f2 && (m3 || m11)) || (f3 && (m2 || m11)) || (f11 && (m2 || m3))) {
            return true;
        }
        if (check_six_type(m, b, b_factors)) {
            return true;
        }
    }
    return false;
}

int main() {
    int b, d;
    scanf("%d %d", &b, &d);
    
    auto b_factors = factorize(b);
    
    // Check 2-type
    if (is_2_type(d, b_factors)) {
        printf("2-type\n");
        printf("%d\n", compute_k(d, b_factors));
        return 0;
    }
    
    // Check 3-type
    if (is_3_type(d, b)) {
        printf("3-type\n");
        return 0;
    }
    
    // Check 11-type
    if (is_11_type(d, b)) {
        printf("11-type\n");
        return 0;
    }
    
    // Check 6-type
    if (check_six_type(d, b, b_factors)) {
        printf("6-type\n");
        return 0;
    }
    
    // Else, 7-type
    printf("7-type\n");
    return 0;
}<#sep#> f(f(f(x)))

But if we had f(f(f(x))) = f(f(x + c)) from x = 0 substitution (with y replaced by x), then the equation becomes:

f(f(x + c)) + f(x) = x + f(c) + f(f(x + c))

Therefore, f(x) = x + f(c), so f(x) = x + d, where d = f(c). Then, substituting back into the original equation gives d = 0.

Therefore, indeed, the only solution is f(x) = x.

But to make sure, let's check for injectivity or surjectivity. Suppose f is injective or surjective, can we get more information?

From the equation f(f(f(y))) = f(f(y + c)) obtained when x = 0. If f is injective, then we can cancel f on both sides, getting f(f(y)) = f(y + c). Then, if f is injective again, we can cancel f again: f(y) = y + c. So then f is linear, f(y) = y + c. Then, substituting back into original equation, as before, we get c = 0, hence f(y) = y. So if f is injective, the only solution is f(y) = y.

But do we know that f is injective?

Suppose f(a) = f(b). Let's see if we can show that a = b.

Let me see. Let's try to check if f is injective.

Suppose f(a) = f(b). Let's set x = t - f(a) and y = a in the original equation. Wait, maybe this approach is too vague. Alternatively, suppose f is not injective, is there a contradiction?

Alternatively, maybe we can prove injectivity from the equation.

Suppose f(a) = f(b). Let's substitute x = a - f(y) into the original equation.

Wait, perhaps another approach. Let me consider setting x = t - f(y) for some t. Then, original equation:

f(f(t - f(y) + f(y))) + f(t - f(y) + y) = t - f(y) + f(f(y)) + f(f(y + f(t - f(y))))

Simplify the left side:

f(f(t)) + f(t - f(y) + y) = t - f(y) + f(f(y)) + f(f(y + f(t - f(y))))

Hmm, not sure. Let's see if f is injective. Suppose f(a) = f(b). Let's substitute y = a and y = b into the equation.

Alternatively, let's consider if f is injective.

Wait, when we substituted x = 0, we had:

f(f(f(y))) = f(f(y + c))

If f is injective, then this implies f(f(y)) = f(y + c). Then, again, if f is injective, f(y) = y + c. So in that case, the function is linear.

But if f is not injective, maybe there are other solutions. Wait, but even if f is not injective, the substitution when y = 0 leads to f(x) = x + d, which is injective. So if f(x) = x + d, then unless d = 0, this is injective, but we found d must be 0, so f(x) = x is injective.

But perhaps the way we derived f(x) = x + d doesn't require injectivity? Let me check again.

From y = 0:

Original equation becomes:

f(f(x + c)) + f(x) = x + f(c) + f(f(f(x)))

But from x = 0 substitution, we had f(f(f(y))) = f(f(y + c)), so f(f(f(x))) = f(f(x + c)). Therefore, substituting back into the equation:

f(f(x + c)) + f(x) = x + f(c) + f(f(x + c))

Cancelling f(f(x + c)) from both sides:

f(x) = x + f(c)

Therefore, this must hold for all x. So f(x) is linear with slope 1 and intercept d = f(c). Therefore, regardless of injectivity, this equation forces f to be linear. Then, substituting back into the original equation forces d = 0, so f(x) = x. So even if we didn't assume injectivity, the steps lead us to f(x) = x.

Therefore, f(x) = x is the only solution.

Let me just check if there's any other possible function. For example, constant functions. Suppose f(x) = k for all x.

Let's test that. Then LHS: f(f(x + f(y))) + f(x + y) = f(k) + k = k + k = 2k.

RHS: x + f(f(y)) + f(f(y + f(x))) = x + f(k) + f(k) = x + k + k = x + 2k.

So 2k = x + 2k, which implies x = 0 for all x. Not possible. So constant functions are not solutions.

Another common function is f(x) = -x. Let's test that.

LHS: f(f(x + f(y))) + f(x + y)

First, f(y) = -y, so x + f(y) = x - y. Then f(f(x + f(y))) = f(x - y) = -(x - y) = -x + y.

Then f(x + y) = -(x + y). So LHS = (-x + y) + (-x - y) = -2x.

RHS: x + f(f(y)) + f(f(y + f(x)))

Compute f(f(y)) = f(-y) = -(-y) = y.

f(x) = -x, so y + f(x) = y - x. Then f(y + f(x)) = f(y - x) = -(y - x) = -y + x. Then f(f(y + f(x))) = f(-y + x) = -(-y + x) = y - x.

So RHS = x + y + (y - x) = x + y + y - x = 2y.

So LHS = -2x and RHS = 2y. For these to be equal for all x, y, we must have -2x = 2y for all x, y. But this is impossible unless x and y are dependent, which they aren't. So f(x) = -x is not a solution.

Therefore, seems like f(x) = x is the only solution.

Another check: suppose f is additive, like f(x) = ax + b. Let's try that.

Let f(x) = ax + b. Then let's substitute into the original equation.

Compute LHS: f(f(x + f(y))) + f(x + y).

First, f(y) = a y + b. Then x + f(y) = x + a y + b.

Then f(x + f(y)) = a(x + a y + b) + b = a x + a² y + a b + b.

Then f(f(x + f(y))) = a(a x + a² y + a b + b) + b = a² x + a³ y + a² b + a b + b.

Then f(x + y) = a(x + y) + b.

So LHS total is [a² x + a³ y + a² b + a b + b] + [a x + a y + b] = a² x + a³ y + a² b + a b + b + a x + a y + b.

Combine like terms:

x terms: a² x + a x = x(a² + a)

y terms: a³ y + a y = y(a³ + a)

constants: a² b + a b + b + b = a² b + a b + 2b

RHS: x + f(f(y)) + f(f(y + f(x)))

Compute f(f(y)) = f(a y + b) = a(a y + b) + b = a² y + a b + b.

f(x) = a x + b, so y + f(x) = y + a x + b.

Then f(y + f(x)) = a(y + a x + b) + b = a y + a² x + a b + b.

Then f(f(y + f(x))) = a(a y + a² x + a b + b) + b = a² y + a³ x + a² b + a b + b.

So RHS is x + [a² y + a b + b] + [a² y + a³ x + a² b + a b + b].

Hmm, maybe I should not get stuck on the examples. Let me think of the steps required to model this problem.

First, model the grid as a grid of characters. Each cell's character defines a sort of vector or direction—the direction the cut takes.

Now, each starting point (letters) is in the grid. Each of these correspond to an order of processing: priority of the letters (A first, B next, etc.).

The key is to find the starting positions of all letters in the grid, ordered by letter (A before B before C, etc). 

Once the list of starts is known (the order in which the cuts start), then for each cut in order:

Need to find the path of the cut itself, to find its starting point (start position, given as the letter's position), and its ending position. The next cut's order will require moving from there to the next start position.

Thus, the problem reduces to:

1. Enumerate all letters in the grid, sorted according to their order (A < B < ... < Z < a < b ... < z). Letters with the same root (e.g., two 'A's) are treated as separate starting points, ordered next to each other in order of processing.

Then, process their own paths. Each cut starts from their start position, and proceeds along its path according to the grid symbols.

Wait error here: Each of these starting points is a single position (for a letter in the grid: e.g., an A may be placed at (x,y)), so each starting point has coordinates (x,y). Then, the head is at that position when starting the cut.

The cut proceeds in its path, moving per the grid characters as it goes. The path can be determined by a traversal through the grid, turning as per direction from the symbols. 

Wait perhaps each symbol in the grid cell defines part of the cut's path. E.g., for a '-' symbol: it's part of a horizontal line East, movement continues in this direction until it hits a direction change (e.g., a '/', which changes direction). The path continues until it reaches an end (which can be a character like > or < which is an end point.

Alternatively, each cell may represent a straight segment or a turn. The path of a cut starts at a letter's position, and follows the direction of the grid's cells until it hits an end symbol (^, <, >, v); then it stops. 

The key is for each starting position (the letters), to traverse the path until the end, which is a cut end symbol (>,v,<,^). 

So for each such path, we can model:

- Starting point (x_start, y_start)

- Ending point (x_end, y_end)

Once we have all cut paths' start and end coordinates in order, then the total distance is the sum between the end of cut[i] and the start of cut[i+1] for each i, except the first's start which is not costed. 

Because the company doesn't count the first distance from resting to the first's start, only the next movements between ends and starts. Wait:

Wait the problem says:

Assume that:

- The company doesn't count distance from resting position to starting point of the first cut.

- IE, the first movement is from the start to first cut's start — not counted. So the distance starts counting from the movement after the first cut.

So for the list of cuts in order: cut1, cut2, cut3... The total distance is the distance between cut1.end and cut2.start, plus between cut2.end and cut3.start, and so on.

So, the first cut's start is not counted (from resting to that start point), but the end of the first cut is the starting point for the distance to the second's start, and onwards.

Thus, to proceed, I need to:

For each cut's path (in letter order):

- Find the sequence of the path from the starting point (the letter's position) until an end symbol (like >, <, etc.) is reached, tracking the current direction.

I learned FORTRAN on a Control Data Corp. 3100 in 1965. Variables starting with 'I' through 'N' were implied to be integers. Ex: 'IGGY' and 'NORB' were integers, 'XMAX' and 'ALPHA' were floating-point. However, you could override this through explicit declaration.

It's interesting that so many people point to summation formulas in math. Certainly i and j are common, but I would say n is as common as i (and m as j if using n instead of i). This makes me think the Fortran answer is also onto something. Some earlier programmers chose the i,j,k over n,m,l and now it's just convention in programming. It seems stickier than summation conventions in math or physics.

-
In math, m and n are more often used as bounds (as in, 0 <= i < m, sigma notation, or dimensions of a matrix / vector space; I'll give you series as an exception). Also look at David's comment to Zack re Lagrange. –  Tobu Dec 7 '10 at 18:54<#sep#>-

#include <bits/stdc++.h>
using namespace std;

struct Fraction {
    int num, den;

    Fraction(int num = 0, int den = 1) {
        if (den == 0) {
            this->num = 0;
            this->den = 1;
            return;
        }
        if (den < 0) {
            num = -num;
            den = -den;
        }
        int g = gcd(abs(num), den);
        this->num = num / g;
        this->den = den / g;
    }

    static int gcd(int a, int b) {
        while (b) {
            a %= b;
            swap(a, b);
        }
        return a;
    }

    bool isZero() const {
        return num == 0;
    }

    Fraction operator-() const {
        return Fraction(-num, den);
    }

    Fraction operator+(const Fraction& other) const {
        return Fraction(num * other.den + other.num * den, den * other.den);
    }

    Fraction operator-(const Fraction& other) const {
        return *this + (-other);
    }

    Fraction operator*(const Fraction& other) const {
        return Fraction(num * other.num, den * other.den);
    }

    Fraction operator/(const Fraction& other) const {
        return Fraction(num * other.den, den * other.num);
    }

    bool operator==(const Fraction& other) const {
        return num == other.num && den == other.den;
    }

    bool operator!=(const Fraction& other) const {
        return !(*this == other);
    }
};

int main() {
    ios_base::sync_with_stdio(false);
    cin.tie(nullptr);

    int m, d;
    cin >> m >> d;

    vector<int> groups(m);
    unordered_map<string, int> group_map;
    int next_group = 1;

    for (int ship = 0; ship < m; ++ship) {
        int ki;
        cin >> ki;

        vector<vector<Fraction>> matrix;
        for (int i = 0; i < ki; ++i) {
            vector<Fraction> row;
            for (int j = 0; j < d; ++j) {
                int a;
                cin >> a;
                row.emplace_back(a, 1);
            }
            matrix.push_back(row);
        }

        int n = ki;
        int rank = 0;

        for (int col = 0; col < d && rank < n; ++col) {
            int pivot_row = -1;
            for (int i = rank; i < n; ++i) {
                if (!matrix[i][col].isZero()) {
                    pivot_row = i;
                    break;
                }
            }

            if (pivot_row == -1) continue;

            swap(matrix[rank], matrix[pivot_row]);

            Fraction pivot = matrix[rank][col];
            for (int j = col; j < d; ++j) {
                matrix[rank][j] = matrix[rank][j] / pivot;
            }

            for (int i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
                if (i != rank && !matrix[i][col].isZero()) {
                    Fraction factor = matrix[i][col];
                    for (int j = col; j < d; ++j) {
                        matrix[i][j] = matrix[i][j] - factor * matrix[rank][j];
                    }
                }
            }

            rank++;
        }

        string key_str;
        for (int i = 0; i < rank; ++i) {
            const auto& row = matrix[i];
            if (!key_str.empty()) {
                key_str += "|";
            }
            for (int j = 0; j < d; ++j) {
                if (j > 0) {
                    key_str += ",";
                }
                key_str += to_string(row[j].num) + "_" + to_string(row[j].den);
            }
        }

        if (group_map.count(key_str)) {
            groups[ship] = group_map[key_str];
        } else {
            group_map[key_str] = next_group;
            groups[ship] = next_group;
            next_group++;
        }
    }

    for (int g : groups) {
        cout << g << ' ';
    }
    cout << endl;

    return 0;
}<#sep#>, so y + f(x) = y + a x + b.

Then f(y + f(x)) = a(y + a x + b) + b = a y + a² x + a b + b.

Then f(f(y + f(x))) = a(a y + a² x + a b + b) + b = a² y + a³ x + a² b + a b + b.

So RHS is x + [a² y + a b + b] + [a² y + a³ x + a² b + a b + b].

Simplify:

x + a² y + a b + b + a² y + a³ x + a² b + a b + b.

Combine like terms:

x terms: x + a³ x = x(1 + a³)

y terms: a² y + a² y = 2a² y

constants: a b + b + a² b + a b + b = 2a b + 2b + a² b

So RHS total is x(1 + a³) + 2a² y + (2a b + 2b + a² b)

Set LHS equal to RHS:

For x terms: a² + a = 1 + a³

For y terms: a³ + a = 2a²

For constants: a² b + a b + 2b = 2a b + 2b + a² b

Let's solve these equations.

First, for y terms:

a³ + a = 2a² ⇒ a³ - 2a² + a = 0 ⇒ a(a² - 2a + 1) = 0 ⇒ a(a - 1)^2 = 0.

So possible a = 0 or a = 1.

If a = 0:

Check x terms: 0 + 0 = 1 + 0 ⇒ 0 = 1, which is false.

If a = 1:

Check x terms: 1 + 1 = 1 + 1 ⇒ 2 = 2, which is true.

Now check constants with a =1:

Left side constants: 1² b + 1*b + 2b = b + b + 2b = 4b

Right side constants: 2*1*b + 2b + 1² b = 2b + 2b + b = 5b

So 4b = 5b ⇒ b = 0.

Therefore, the only additive solution is a=1 and b=0, so f(x) = x. Which matches our previous conclusion.

Therefore, even if we assume additivity, we only get f(x)=x. Since our previous steps didn't assume additivity and still forced f to be linear, it's safe to conclude that f(x)=x is the only solution.

Thus, the answer is f(x) = x.

**Final Answer**
The solution is \boxed{f(x) = x}.
</think>To solve the functional equation
\[ f(f(x + f(y))) + f(x + y) = x + f(f(y)) + f(f(y + f(x))) \]
for all real numbers \( x \) and \( y \), we proceed as follows:

1. **Substitute \( x = 0 \)**:
   \[
   f(f(f(y))) + f(y) = f(f(y)) + f(f(y + f(0)))
   \]
   Let \( c = f(0) \). This simplifies to:
   \[
   f(f(f(y))) = f(f(y + c))
   \]

2. **Substitute \( y = 0 \)**:
   \[
   f(f(x + c)) + f(x) = x + f(c) + f(f(f(x)))
   \]
   Using the result from \( x = 0 \), we know \( f(f(f(x))) = f(f(x + c)) \). Substituting this in, we get:
   \[
   f(f(x + c)) + f(x) = x + f(c) + f(f(x + c))
   \]
   Subtracting \( f(f(x + c)) \) from both sides, we find:
   \[
   f(x) = x + f(c)
   \]
   Let \( d = f(c) \), so \( f(x) = x + d \).

3. **Verify \( f(x) = x + d \)**:
   Substitute \( f(x) = x + d \) into the original equation:
   \[
   f(f(x + f(y))) + f(x + y) = x + f(f(y)) + f(f(y + f(x)))
   \]
   Simplifying each term:
   \[
   f(f(x + f(y))) = f(f(x + y + d)) = f(x + y + 2d) = x + y + 3d
   \]
   \[
   f(x + y) = x + y + d
   \]
   \[
   f(f(y)) = f(y + d) = y + 2d
   \]
   \[
   f(f(y + f(x))) = f(f(y + x + d)) = f(y + x + 2d) = y + x + 3d
   \]
   Substituting these into the original equation:
   \[
   (x + y + 3d) + (x + y + d) = x + (y + 2d) + (y + x + 3d)
   \]
   Simplifying both sides:
   \[
   2x + 2y + 4d = 2x + 2y + 5d
   \]
   This implies:
   \[
   4d = 5d \implies d = 0
   \]
   Therefore, \( f(x) = x \).

4. **Conclusion**:
   The only solution to the functional equation is:
   \[
   \boxed{f(x) = x}

#include <iostream>
#include <vector>
#include <cstdlib>
#include <iomanip>
using namespace std;

struct Node {
    long long val;
    int priority;
    long long sum;
    int count;
    Node *left, *right;
    Node(long long v) : val(v), priority(rand()), sum(v), count(1), left(nullptr), right(nullptr) {}
    void update() {
        sum = val;
        count = 1;
        if (left) {
            sum += left->sum;
            count += left->count;
        }
        if (right) {
            sum += right->sum;
            count += right->count;
        }
    }
};

pair<Node*, Node*> split(Node* root, long long key) {
    if (!root) return {nullptr, nullptr};
    if (root->val < key) {
        auto [left, right] = split(root->right, key);
        root->right = left;
        root->update();
        return {root, right};
    } else {
        auto [left, right] = split(root->left, key);
        root->left = right;
        root->update();
        return {left, root};
    }
}

Node* merge(Node* a, Node* b) {
    if (!a) return b;
    if (!b) return a;
    if (a->priority > b->priority) {
        a->right = merge(a->right, b);
        a->update();
        return a;
    } else {
        b->left = merge(a, b->left);
        b->update();
        return b;
    }
}

void insert(Node*& root, long long val) {
    auto [left, right] = split(root, val);
    root = merge(merge(left, new Node(val)), right);
}

void remove(Node*& root, long long val) {
    auto [left, temp] = split(root, val);
    auto [toRemove, right] = split(temp, val + 1);
    delete toRemove;
    root = merge(left, right);
}

pair<long long, long long> get_less_sum_count(Node* root, long long L) {
    auto [left, right] = split(root, L);
    long long sum = left ? left->sum : 0;
    int count = left ? left->count : 0;
    root = merge(left, right);
    return {sum, count};
}

int main() {
    ios_base::sync_with_stdio(false);
    cin.tie(nullptr);
    cout << fixed << setprecision(5);

    int n, q;
    cin >> n >> q;
    vector<long long> h(n);
    Node* root = nullptr;
    for (int i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
        cin >> h[i];
        insert(root, h[i]);
    }

    while (q--) {
        int type;
        cin >> type;
        if (type == 1) {
            int pi;
            long long xi;
            cin >> pi >> xi;
            pi--;
            remove(root, h[pi]);
            h[pi] = xi;
            insert(root, h[pi]);
        } else {
            long long vi;
            cin >> vi;
            double low = 0, high = 1e18;
            for (int iter = 0; iter < 100; ++iter) {
                double mid = (low + high) / 2;
                auto [sum_less, count_less] = get_less_sum_count(root, mid);
                double s = mid * count_less - sum_less;
                if (s >= vi) {
                    high = mid;
                } else {
                    low = mid;
                }
            }
            cout << high << '\n';
        }
    }

    return 0;

</think>To find the value of \(A_{50}\) in the equation

\[
\frac{100!}{x(x+1)(x+2)\cdots (x+100)} = \frac{A_0}{x} + \frac{A_1}{x+1} + \frac{A_2}{x+2} + \cdots + \frac{A_{100}}{x+100},
\]

we use the cover-up method for partial fraction decomposition. The coefficient \(A_{50}\) can be found by evaluating the left-hand side at \(x = -50\) after removing the factor \((x + 50)\) from the denominator. This gives us:

\[
A_{50} = \frac{100!}{(-50)(-49)\cdots(-1)(1)(2)\cdots(50)}.
\]

The denominator simplifies as follows:
- The terms \((-50), (-49), \ldots, (-1)\) form a product of 50 negative terms, which is \((-1)^{50} \cdot 50!\).
- The terms \((1), (2), \ldots, (50)\) form a product of 50 positive terms, which is \(50!\).

Thus, the denominator is:

\[
(-1)^{50} \cdot 50! \cdot 50! = (50!)^2.
\]

Therefore, we have:

\[
A_{50} = \frac{100!}{(50!)^2}.
\]

This expression is the binomial coefficient \(\binom{100}{50}\), so:

\[
A_{50} = \binom{100}{50}.
\]

The value of \(A_{50}\) is:

\[
\boxed{\binom{100}{50}}.

#include <bits/stdc++.h>
using namespace std;

const int INF = 1e9;

int main() {
    ios_base::sync_with_stdio(false);
    cin.tie(nullptr);

    int n, c;
    cin >> n >> c;

    vector<int> left_min(n + 2, INF);
    vector<int> left_max(n + 2, -INF);
    vector<int> right_min(n + 2, INF);
    vector<int> right_max(n + 2, -INF);

    for (int i = 0; i < c; ++i) {
        int a, b;
        string dir;
        cin >> a >> b >> dir;
        if (dir == "LEFT") {
            if (b < left_min[a]) left_min[a] = b;
            if (b > left_max[a]) left_max[a] = b;
        } else {
            if (b < right_min[a]) right_min[a] = b;
            if (b > right_max[a]) right_max[a] = b;
        }
    }

    vector<int> left_child(n + 2, 0);
    vector<int> right_child(n + 2, 0);
    vector<int> size(n + 2, 0);
    bool impossible = false;

    for (int ai = n; ai >= 1; --ai) {
        bool has_left = (left_min[ai] != INF);
        int current_left_size = 0;

        if (has_left) {
            left_child[ai] = ai + 1;
            if (ai + 1 > n) {
                impossible = true;
            } else {
                current_left_size = size[ai + 1];
                if (left_min[ai] < ai + 1 || left_max[ai] > ai + 1 + current_left_size - 1) {
                    impossible = true;
                }
            }
        }

        bool has_right = (right_min[ai] != INF);
        int current_right_size = 0;

        if (has_right) {
            if (has_left) {
                right_child[ai] = ai + 1 + current_left_size;
            } else {
                right_child[ai] = ai + 1;
            }

            if (right_child[ai] > n) {
                impossible = true;
            } else {
                current_right_size = size[right_child[ai]];
                if (right_min[ai] < right_child[ai] || right_max[ai] > right_child[ai] + current_right_size - 1) {
                    impossible = true;
                }
            }
        }

        size[ai] = 1 + current_left_size + current_right_size;

        if (ai == 1 && size[ai] != n) {
            impossible = true;
        }

        if (impossible) break;
    }

    if (impossible) {
        cout << "IMPOSSIBLE\n";
        return 0;
    }

    vector<int> in_order;
    stack<pair<int, bool>> s;
    s.push({1, false});

    while (!s.empty()) {
        auto [node, visited] = s.top();
        s.pop();

        if (!visited) {
            if (right_child[node] != 0) {
                s.push({right_child[node], false});
            }
            s.push({node, true});
            if (left_child[node] != 0) {
                s.push({left_child[node], false});
            }
        } else {
            in_order.push_back(node);
        }
    }

    for (size_t i = 0; i < in_order.size(); ++i) {
        if (i > 0) cout << " ";
        cout << in_order[i];
    }
    cout << "\n";
    return 0;

104. ## economics

apples cost £2 and pears cost £4. For John the marginal Utility (MU) of the<#sep#>-

#include <bits/stdc++.h>
using namespace std;

typedef long long ll;

struct SwapInfo {
    ll s;
    int i, j;
    bool operator<(const SwapInfo& other) const {
        return s < other.s;
    }
};

int main() {
    ios::sync_with_stdio(false);
    cin.tie(0);

    int n, m;
    cin >> n;
    vector<ll> a(n);
    for (int i = 0; i < n; ++i) cin >> a[i];
    cin >> m;
    vector<ll> b(m);
    for (int j = 0; j < m; ++j) cin >> b[j];

    ll sum_a = accumulate(a.begin(), a.end(), 0LL);
    ll sum_b = accumulate(b.begin(), b.end(), 0LL);
    ll D = sum_a - sum_b;
    ll best_v = abs(D);
    vector<pair<int, int>> swaps;

    // Check 0 swaps
    int best_k = 0;

    // Check 1 swap
    vector<SwapInfo> swaps_one;
    for (int i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
        for (int j = 0; j < m; ++j) {
            swaps_one.push_back({a[i] - b[j], i, j});
        }
    }
    ll best_one_v = best_v;
    pair<int, int> best_swap;
    for (auto& si : swaps_one) {
        ll v = abs(D - 2 * si.s);
        if (v < best_one_v) {
            best_one_v = v;
            best_swap = {si.i, si.j};
        }
    }

    if (best_one_v < best_v) {
        best_v = best_one_v;
        best_k = 1;
        swaps = {{best_swap.first + 1, best_swap.second + 1}};
    }

    // Check 2 swaps
    vector<SwapInfo> swaps_two;
    for (int i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
        for (int j = 0; j < m; ++j) {
            swaps_two.push_back({a[i] - b[j], i, j});
        }
    }
    sort(swaps_two.begin(), swaps_two.end());

    ll target_sum = D;
    bool found = false;
    pair<int, int> s1, s2;
    ll best_two_v = best_v;

    for (size_t i = 0; i < swaps_two.size(); ++i) {
        ll s1_val = swaps_two[i].s;
        ll desired = target_sum - s1_val;
        int idx = lower_bound(swaps_two.begin(), swaps_two.end(), SwapInfo{desired, 0, 0}) - swaps_two.begin();

        for (int k = max(0, idx - 2); k < min((int)swaps_two.size(), idx + 3); ++k) {
            if (i == k) continue;
            auto& si_i = swaps_two[i];
            auto& si_k = swaps_two[k];
            ll sum_s = si_i.s + si_k.s;
            ll current_v = abs(D - 2 * sum_s);

            bool same_swap = (si_i.i == si_k.i && si_i.j == si_k.j);
            if (same_swap) continue;

            if (current_v < best_two_v) {
                best_two_v = current_v;
                s1 = {si_i.i + 1, si_i.j + 1};
                s2 = {si_k.i + 1, si_k.j + 1};
                found = true;
            } else if (current_v == best_two_v && !found) {
                s1 = {si_i.i + 1, si_i.j + 1};
                s2 = {si_k.i + 1, si_k.j + 1};
                found = true;
            }
        }
    }

    if (best_two_v < best_v) {
        best_v = best_two_v;
        best_k = 2;
        swaps = {s1, s2};
    }

    // Output
    cout << best_v << '\n' << best_k << '\n';
    for (auto& p : swaps) {
        cout << p.first << ' ' << p.second << '\n';
    }

    return 0;

Therefore, if we can compute the sum from i=0 to n, then we can subtract C(2n, n) to get S.

So the problem reduces to finding sum_{i=0}^n C(2i, i)C(2(n -i), n -i).

Suppose we have:

sum_{i=0}^n C(2i, i)C(2(n - i), n - i) = 4^n (from generating functions).

But this sum can also be written as C(2n, n) [when i=0] + C(2n, n) [when i=n] + sum_{i=1}^{n-1} C(2i, i)C(2(n -i), n -i).

4^n = 2*C(2n, n) + (S - C(2n, n))

Therefore:

4^n = C(2n, n) + S

Thus, solving for S:

S = 4^n - C(2n, n)

4^1 - C(2,1) = 4 - 2 = 2. Which matches. Good.

Therefore, the general formula is S = 4^n - binomial(2n, n). Therefore, the simplified sum is 4^n minus the central binomial coefficient.

**Final Answer**
The simplified sum is \boxed{4^n - \binom{2n}{n}}.

def next_direction(prev_dir, current_symbol):

prev_dir is the direction coming into this cell.

current_symbol determines the new direction.

 The 90-degree corners (e.g., ┌,┐) turn 90 degrees from the incoming direction.

 For ex:

东 East (1, 0)

 South (0,1)

 West (-1, 0)

 North (0,-1)

 Alternatively, we can model direction as:

 Straight segments and corners turning by 90 or 45.

 Alternatively, each cell's symbol determines the valid directions it can come from and must go to.

 This is getting too complex.

 Perhaps the following convention:

 For each cell's symbol:

 The symbol determines the allowed direction of the path through it.

 For example, 

 A ‘-- symbol只能 be traversed horizontally, so the path came from the west or east, and continues in the other.

 Thus, if passed by from east (coming from East), then one exits to the west, and vice versa.

+Authenticate using an OTP / One Time Password (Two-Factor authentication)
+^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^
+
+Use OTP through Python code
+"""""""""""""""""""""""""""
+
+``creodias`` needs a temporary 6-digits code to authenticate in addition of the ``username`` and ``password``. Check
+`Creodias documentation <https://creodias.docs.cloudferro.com/en/latest/gettingstarted/Two-Factor-Authentication-for-Creodias-Site.html>`_
+to see how to get this code once you are registered. This OTP code will only be valid for a few seconds, so you will
+better set it dynamically in your python code instead of storing it statically in your user configuration file.
+
+Set the OTP by updating ``creodias`` credentials for ``totp`` entry, using one of the two following configuration update
+commands:
+
+.. code-block:: python
+
+   dag.providers_config["creodias"].auth.credentials["totp"] = "PLEASE_CHANGE_ME"
+
+   # OR
+
+   dag.update_providers_config(
+      """
+      creodias:
+         auth:
+            credentials:
+               totp: PLEASE_CHANGE_ME
+      """
+   )
+
+Then quickly authenticate as this OTP has a few seconds only lifetime. First authentication will retrieve a token that
+will be stored and used if further authentication tries fail:
+
+.. code-block:: python
+
+   dag._plugins_manager.get_auth_plugin("creodias").authenticate()
+
+Please note that authentication mechanism is already included in
+`download methods <https://eodag.readthedocs.io/en/stable/notebooks/api_user_guide/7_download.html>`_ , so you could also directly execute a
+download to retrieve the token while the OTP is still valid.
+
+Use OTP through CLI
+"""""""""""""""""""
+
+To download through CLI a product on a provider that needs a One Time Password,  e.g. ``creodias``, first search on this
+provider (increase the provider priotity to make eodag search on it):
+
+.. code-block:: console
+
+        EODAG__CREODIAS__PRIORITY=2 eodag search -b 1 43 2 44 -s 2018-01-01 -e 2018-01-31 -p S2_MSI_L1C --items 1
+
+Then download using the OTP (``creodias`` needs it as ``totp`` parameter):
+
+.. code-block:: console
+
+        EODAG__CREODIAS__AUTH__CREDENTIALS__TOTP=PLEASE_CHANGE_ME eodag download --search-results search_results.geojson
+
+If needed, check in the documentation how to
+`use environment variables to configure EODAG <#environment-variable-configuration>`_.

#include <bits/stdc++.h>
using namespace std;

const int MOD = 998244353;

struct BipartiteDSU {
    vector<int> parent, rank, parity;
    BipartiteDSU(int n) {
        parent.resize(n + 1);
        rank.resize(n + 1, 0);
        parity.resize(n + 1, 0);
        for (int i = 0; i <= n; ++i) parent[i] = i;
    }
    
    pair<int, int> find(int u) {
        if (parent[u] != u) {
            auto [p, pp] = find(parent[u]);
            parent[u] = p;
            parity[u] ^= pp;
        }
        return {parent[u], parity[u]};
    }
    
    bool merge(int u, int v, int w) {
        auto [pu, cu] = find(u);
        auto [pv, cv] = find(v);
        if (pu == pv) {
            return (cu ^ cv ^ w) == 0;
        }
        if (rank[pu] < rank[pv]) {
            swap(pu, pv);
            swap(cu, cv);
        }
        parent[pv] = pu;
        parity[pv] = cu ^ cv ^ w;
        if (rank[pu] == rank[pv]) ++rank[pu];
        return true;
    }
};

int main() {
    ios_base::sync_with_stdio(false);
    cin.tie(nullptr);
    
    int n1, n2, m;
    cin >> n1 >> n2 >> m;
    int total_nodes = n1 + n2;
    BipartiteDSU dsu(total_nodes);
    vector<bool> is_red(m + 1, false);
    vector<bool> is_cycle(m + 1, false);
    vector<pair<int, int>> edges;
    edges.reserve(m);
    for (int i = 0; i < m; ++i) {
        int x, y;
        cin >> x >> y;
        y += n1;
        edges.emplace_back(x, y);
    }
    
    int hash = 0;
    vector<int> pow2(m + 2);
    pow2[0] = 1;
    for (int i = 1; i <= m + 1; ++i) {
        pow2[i] = (pow2[i-1] * 2) % MOD;
    }
    
    for (int i = 0; i < m; ++i) {
        auto [u, v] = edges[i];
        auto [pu, cu] = dsu.find(u);
        auto [pv, cv] = dsu.find(v);
        if (pu != pv) {
            dsu.merge(u, v, 1);
            is_red[i+1] = true;
            hash = (hash + pow2[i+1]) % MOD;
        } else {
            if ((cu ^ cv) == 0) {
                is_cycle[i+1] = true;
                hash = (hash + pow2[i+1]) % MOD;
            }
        }
    }
    
    int q;
    cin >> q;
    vector<pair<int, int>> added_edges;
    vector<int> last_hash;
    while (q--) {
        int type;
        cin >> type;
        if (type == 1) {
            int v1, v2;
            cin >> v1 >> v2;
            v2 += n1;
            edges.emplace_back(v1, v2);
            int idx = edges.size();
            auto [u, v] = edges.back();
            auto [pu, cu] = dsu.find(u);
            auto [pv, cv] = dsu.find(v);
            if (pu != pv) {
                dsu.merge(u, v, 1);
                is_red.push_back(true);
                hash = (hash + pow2[idx]) % MOD;
                added_edges.emplace_back(idx, 0);
            } else {
                if ((cu ^ cv) == 0) {
                    is_cycle.push_back(true);
                    hash = (hash + pow2[idx]) % MOD;
                    added_edges.emplace_back(idx, 1);
                } else {
                    added_edges.emplace_back(idx, 2);
                }
            }
            cout << hash << endl;
            last_hash.push_back(hash);
        } else {
            vector<int> red_edges;
            for (int i = 1; i <= m; ++i) {
                if (is_cycle[i] || (i <= (int)is_red.size() && is_red[i])) {
                    red_edges.push_back(i);
                }
            }
            for (auto [idx, t] : added_edges) {
                if (t == 0 || t == 1) {
                    red_edges.push_back(idx);
                }
            }
            cout << red_edges.size();
            for (int e : red_edges) {
                cout << ' ' << e;
            }
            cout << endl;
        }
        fflush(stdout);
    }
    
    return 0;
}<#sep#>4^n - binomial(2n, n). Therefore, the simplified sum is 4^n minus the central binomial coefficient.

**Final Answer**
The simplified sum is \boxed{4^n - \binom{2n}{n}}.
</think>To simplify the sum \( S = \sum_{i=1}^{n} \binom{2i}{i} \binom{2n-2i}{n-i} \), we use generating functions and properties of convolutions.

1. **Generating Functions**:
   The generating function for the central binomial coefficient \(\binom{2i}{i}\) is given by:
   \[
   G(x) = \sum_{i=0}^{\infty} \binom{2i}{i} x^i = \frac{1}{\sqrt{1 - 4x}}
   \]

2. **Convolution of Generating Functions**:
   The generating function for the convolution of two central binomial coefficients is:
   \[
   G(x)^2 = \left( \frac{1}{\sqrt{1 - 4x}} \right)^2 = \frac{1}{1 - 4x}
   \]
   The series expansion of \(\frac{1}{1 - 4x}\) is:
   \[
   \frac{1}{1 - 4x} = \sum_{n=0}^{\infty} 4^n x^n
   \]
   This implies that the coefficient of \(x^n\) in the expansion is \(4^n\).

3. **Full Convolution Sum**:
   The full convolution sum from \(i = 0\) to \(n\) is:
   \[
   \sum_{i=0}^{n} \binom{2i}{i} \binom{2n-2i}{n-i} = 4^n
   \]
   This sum includes the terms at \(i = 0\) and \(i = n\), each contributing \(\binom{2n}{n}\):
   \[
   \sum_{i=0}^{n} \binom{2i}{i} \binom{2n-2i}{n-i} = \binom{2n}{n} + \sum_{i=1}^{n-1} \binom{2i}{i} \binom{2n-2i}{n-i} + \binom{2n}{n}
   \]
   Simplifying, we get:
   \[
   4^n = 2 \binom{2n}{n} + \sum_{i=1}^{n-1} \binom{2i}{i} \binom{2n-2i}{n-i}
   \]

4. **Adjusting for the Original Sum \(S\)**:
   The original sum \(S\) starts at \(i = 1\) and includes the term at \(i = n\):
   \[
   S = \sum_{i=1}^{n} \binom{2i}{i} \binom{2n-2i}{n-i}
   \]
   Thus, we have:
   \[
   4^n = \binom{2n}{n} + S
   \]
   Solving for \(S\), we get:
   \[
   S = 4^n - \binom{2n}{n}
   \]

Therefore, the simplified sum is:
\[
\boxed{4^n - \binom{2n}{n}}
\]<#sep#>

#include <bits/stdc++.h>
using namespace std;

int compute_size_u(const vector<unordered_set<int>>& adj, const unordered_set<int>& T, int u, int v) {
    unordered_set<int> visited;
    queue<int> q;
    q.push(u);
    visited.insert(u);
    int count = 0;

    while (!q.empty()) {
        int x = q.front();
        q.pop();
        count++;
        for (int y : adj[x]) {
            if (!T.count(y)) continue;
            if ((x == u && y == v) || (x == v && y == u)) continue;
            if (!visited.count(y)) {
                visited.insert(y);
                q.push(y);
            }
        }
    }
    return count;
}

unordered_set<int> get_new_T(const vector<unordered_set<int>>& adj, const unordered_set<int>& current_T, int a, int u_edge, int v_edge) {
    unordered_set<int> new_T;
    queue<int> q;
    q.push(a);
    new_T.insert(a);

    while (!q.empty()) {
        int x = q.front();
        q.pop();
        for (int y : adj[x]) {
            if (!current_T.count(y)) continue;
            if ((x == u_edge && y == v_edge) || (x == v_edge && y == u_edge)) continue;
            if (!new_T.count(y)) {
                new_T.insert(y);
                q.push(y);
            }
        }
    }

    return new_T;
}

int main() {
    int n;
    cin >> n;

    vector<unordered_set<int>> adj(n + 1);
    vector<pair<int, int>> edges;

    for (int i = 0; i < n - 1; ++i) {
        int u, v;
        cin >> u >> v;
        adj[u].insert(v);
        adj[v].insert(u);
        edges.emplace_back(u, v);
    }

    unordered_set<int> current_T;
    for (int i = 1; i <= n; ++i) current_T.insert(i);

    while (current_T.size() > 1) {
        vector<pair<int, int>> possible_edges;
        for (auto& e : edges) {
            int u = e.first, v = e.second;
            if (current_T.count(u) && current_T.count(v)) {
                possible_edges.push_back(e);
            }
        }

        pair<int, int> best_edge;
        int min_max = n + 1;

        for (auto& e : possible_edges) {
            int u = e.first, v = e.second;
            int size_u = compute_size_u(adj, current_T, u, v);
            int max_size = max(size_u, (int)current_T.size() - size_u);
            if (max_size < min_max) {
                min_max = max_size;
                best_edge = e;
            } else if (max_size == min_max && best_edge == pair<int, int>{0, 0}) {
                best_edge = e;
            }
        }

        int u = best_edge.first, v = best_edge.second;
        cout << "? " << u << " " << v << endl;
        cout.flush();

        int a;
        cin >> a;

        current_T = get_new_T(adj, current_T, a, u, v);
    }

    cout << "! " << *current_T.begin() << endl;
    cout.flush();

    return 0;
}<#sep#>Given the function \( f(x) = x^2 \), find the image of the point \((0,0)\).

#include <iostream>
using namespace std;

int n;

int query(int x1, int y1, int x2, int y2) {
    cout << "? " << x1 << ' ' << y1 << ' ' << x2 << ' ' << y2 << endl;
    fflush(stdout);
    int res;
    cin >> res;
    return res;
}

int find_min_x() {
    int low = 1, high = n, ans = 0;
    while (low <= high) {
        int mid = (low + high) / 2;
        if (query(mid, 1, n, n) == 2) {
            ans = mid;
            low = mid + 1;
        } else {
            high = mid - 1;
        }
    }
    return ans;
}

int find_max_x() {
    int low = 1, high = n, ans = 0;
    while (low <= high) {
        int mid = (low + high) / 2;
        if (query(1, 1, mid, n) == 2) {
            ans = mid;
            high = mid - 1;
        } else {
            low = mid + 1;
        }
    }
    return ans;
}

int find_min_y() {
    int low = 1, high = n, ans = 0;
    while (low <= high) {
        int mid = (low + high) / 2;
        if (query(1, mid, n, n) == 2) {
            ans = mid;
            low = mid + 1;
        } else {
            high = mid - 1;
        }
    }
    return ans;
}

int find_max_y() {
    int low = 1, high = n, ans = 0;
    while (low <= high) {
        int mid = (low + high) / 2;
        if (query(1, 1, n, mid) == 2) {
            ans = mid;
            high = mid - 1;
        } else {
            low = mid + 1;
        }
    }
    return ans;
}

int find_split_x(int min_x, int max_x, int min_y, int max_y) {
    int low = min_x, high = max_x - 1, ans = -1;
    while (low <= high) {
        int mid = (low + high) / 2;
        int res = query(min_x, min_y, mid, max_y);
        if (res == 1) {
            ans = mid;
            low = mid + 1;
        } else {
            high = mid - 1;
        }
    }
    return ans;
}

int find_split_y(int min_y, int max_y, int min_x, int max_x) {
    int low = min_y, high = max_y - 1, ans = -1;
    while (low <= high) {
        int mid = (low + high) / 2;
        int res = query(min_x, min_y, max_x, mid);
        if (res == 1) {
            ans = mid;
            low = mid + 1;
        } else {
            high = mid - 1;
        }
    }
    return ans;
}

int find_y1(int x1, int x2, int max_y, int min_y) {
    int low = min_y, high = max_y, ans = min_y;
    while (low <= high) {
        int mid = (low + high) / 2;
        if (query(x1, mid, x2, max_y) == 1) {
            ans = mid;
            low = mid + 1;
        } else {
            high = mid - 1;
        }
    }
    return ans;
}

int find_y2(int x1, int x2, int min_y, int max_y) {
    int low = min_y, high = max_y, ans = max_y;
    while (low <= high) {
        int mid = (low + high) / 2;
        if (query(x1, min_y, x2, mid) == 1) {
            ans = mid;
            high = mid - 1;
        } else {
            low = mid + 1;
        }
    }
    return ans;
}

int find_x1(int y1, int y2, int max_x, int min_x) {
    int low = min_x, high = max_x, ans = min_x;
    while (low <= high) {
        int mid = (low + high) / 2;
        if (query(mid, y1, max_x, y2) == 1) {
            ans = mid;
            low = mid + 1;
        } else {
            high = mid - 1;
        }
    }
    return ans;
}

int find_x2(int y1, int y2, int min_x, int max_x) {
    int low = min_x, high = max_x, ans = max_x;
    while (low <= high) {
        int mid = (low + high) / 2;
        if (query(min_x, y1, mid, y2) == 1) {
            ans = mid;
            high = mid - 1;
        } else {
            low = mid + 1;
        }
    }
    return ans;
}

int main() {
    cin >> n;
    int min_x = find_min_x();
    int max_x = find_max_x();
    int min_y = find_min_y();
    int max_y = find_max_y();
    
    int split_x = find_split_x(min_x, max_x, min_y, max_y);
    if (split_x != -1) {
        int y1_left = find_y1(min_x, split_x, max_y, min_y);
        int y2_left = find_y2(min_x, split_x, min_y, max_y);
        int y1_right = find_y1(split_x + 1, max_x, max_y, min_y);
        int y2_right = find_y2(split_x + 1, max_x, min_y, max_y);
        cout << "! " << min_x << ' ' << y1_left << ' ' << split_x << ' ' << y2_left << ' '
             << split_x + 1 << ' ' << y1_right << ' ' << max_x << ' ' << y2_right << endl;
    } else {
        int split_y = find_split_y(min_y, max_y, min_x, max_x);
        int x1_lower = find_x1(min_y, split_y, max_x, min_x);
        int x2_lower = find_x2(min_y, split_y, min_x, max_x);
        int x1_upper = find_x1(split_y + 1, max_y, max_x, min_x);
        int x2_upper = find_x2(split_y + 1, max_y, min_x, max_x);
        cout << "! " << x1_lower << ' ' << min_y << ' ' << x2_lower << ' ' << split_y << ' '
             << x1_upper << ' ' << split_y + 1 << ' ' << x2_upper << ' ' << max_y << endl;
    }
    fflush(stdout);
    return 0;

1. **Identify the function and the point**: The function given is \( f(x) = x^2 \), and the point is \((0,0)\).

2. **Apply the function to the x-coordinate**: The function \( f(x) = x^2 \) maps the x-coordinate of the point to \( f(x) \). For the point \((0,0)\), the x-coordinate is \(0\).

3. **Calculate the image of the x-coordinate**:

   f(0) = 0^2 = 0
   \]

When searching for Sentinel-1 GRD products with CreoDIAS as a provider, the `download` command returns an error.

It seems than CreoDIAS has recently updated their website (see new website "[CreoDIAS 2.0](https://creodias.eu/)" that now requires MFA to sign in) and their authentification method through the API.

