## Oscillator

An electronic circuit that generates a periodic output, often a sinusoid or a square wave. Oscillators have a wide range of applications in electronic circuits: they are used, for example, to produce the so-called clock signals that synchronize the internal operations of all computers; they produce and decode radio signals; they produce the scanning signals for television tubes; they keep time in electronic wristwatches; and they can be used to convert signals from transducers into a readily transmitted form.

Oscillators may be constructed in many ways, but they always contain certain types of elements. They need a power supply, a frequency-determining element or circuit, a positive-feedback circuit or device (to prevent a zero output), and a nonlinearity (to define the output-signal amplitude). Different choices for these elements give different oscillator circuits with different properties and applications.

Oscillators are broadly divided into relaxation and quasilinear classes. Relaxation oscillators use strong nonlinearities, such as switching elements, and their internal signals tend to have sharp edges and sudden changes in slope; often these signals are square waves, trapezoids, or triangle waves. The quasilinear oscillators, on the other hand, tend to contain smooth sinusoidal signals because they regulate amplitude with weak nonlinearities. The type of signal appearing internally does not always determine the application, since it is possible to convert between sine and square waves. Relaxation oscillators are often simpler to design and more flexible, while the nearly linear types dominate when precise control of frequency is important.

#### Relaxation oscillators

Illustration a shows a simple operational-amplifier based relaxation oscillator. This circuit can be understood in a number of ways (for example, as a negative-resistance circuit), but its operation can be followed by studying the signals at its nodes (illus. b). The two resistors, labeled r, provide a positive-feedback path that forces the amplifier output to saturate at the largest possible (either positive or negative) output voltage. If v+, for example, is initially slightly greater than v-, then the amplifier action increases vo, which in turn further increases v+ through the two resistors labelled r. This loop continues to operate, increasing vo until the operational amplifier saturates at some value Vmax. [An operational amplifier ideally follows Eq.

(1)
(1), where Av is very large, but is restricted to output levels &verbar;vo&verbar; ≤ Vmax.] For the purposes of analyzing the circuit, the waveforms in the illustration have been drawn with the assumption that this mechanism has already operated at time 0 and that the initial charge on the capacitor is zero. See Amplifier, Operational amplifier

Capacitor C will now slowly change from vo through resistor R, toward Vmax, according to Eq. (2). (2)  Up until time t1, this process continues without any change in the amplifier's output because v+ > v-, and so vo = Vmax. At t1, however, v+ = v- and vo will start to decrease. This causes v+ to drop, and the positive-feedback action now drives the amplifier output negative until vo = -Vmax. Capacitor C now discharges exponentially toward the new output voltage until once again, at time t2, v+ = v-, and the process starts again. The period of oscillation for this circuit is 2RC ln 3.

The basic elements of an oscillator that were mentioned above are all clearly visible in this circuit. Two direct-current power supplies are implicit in the diagram (the operational amplifier will not work without them), the RC circuit sets frequency, there is a resistive positive-feedback path that makes the mathematical possibility vo(t) = 0 unstable, and the saturation behavior of the amplifier sets the amplitude of oscillation at the output to ±Vmax.

Relaxation oscillators that have a low duty cycle—that is, produce output pulses whose durations are a small fraction of the overall period—are sometimes called blocking oscillators because their operation is characterized by an “on” transient that “blocks” itself, followed by a recovery period.

Inverters (digital circuits that invert a logic signal, so that a 0 at the input produces a 1 at the output, and vice versa) are essentially voltage amplifiers and can be used to make relaxation oscillators in a number of ways. A circuit related to that of the illustration uses a loop of two inverters and a capacitor C to provide positive feedback, with a resistor R in parallel with<#sep#>-

#include <bits/stdc++.h>
using namespace std;

const int MAXN = 3e5 + 10;
const int LOG = 20;

vector<int> adj[MAXN];
int up[LOG][MAXN];
int depth[MAXN];
string s;

void dfs(int u, int p) {
    up[0][u] = p;
    for (int i = 1; i < LOG; ++i) {
        up[i][u] = (up[i-1][u] == -1) ? -1 : up[i-1][up[i-1][u]];
    }
    for (int v : adj[u]) {
        if (v != p) {
            depth[v] = depth[u] + 1;
            dfs(v, p);
        }
    }
}

int lca(int u, int v) {
    if (depth[u] < depth[v]) swap(u, v);
    for (int i = LOG-1; i >= 0; --i) {
        if (depth[u] - (1 << i) >= depth[v]) {
            u = up[i][u];
        }
    }
    if (u == v) return u;
    for (int i = LOG-1; i >= 0; --i) {
        if (up[i][u] != up[i][v]) {
            u = up[i][u];
            v = up[i][v];
        }
    }
    return up[0][u];
}

int get_kth(int u, int k) {
    for (int i = 0; i < LOG; ++i) {
        if (k & (1 << i)) {
            u = up[i][u];
            if (u == -1) return -1;
        }
    }
    return u;
}

int main() {
    ios_base::sync_with_stdio(false);
    cin.tie(0);
    
    int n;
    cin >> n;
    cin >> s;
    for (int i = 0; i < n-1; ++i) {
        int u, v;
        cin >> u >> v;
        adj[u].push_back(v);
        adj[v].push_back(u);
    }
    memset(up, -1, sizeof(up));
    depth[1] = 0;
    dfs(1, -1);
    
    int m;
    cin >> m;
    while (m--) {
        int a, b, c, d;
        cin >> a >> b >> c >> d;
        
        int l_ab = lca(a, b);
        int up_len_ab = depth[a] - depth[l_ab] + 1;
        int down_len_ab = depth[b] - depth[l_ab];
        int len_ab = up_len_ab + down_len_ab;
        
        int l_cd = lca(c, d);
        int up_len_cd = depth[c] - depth[l_cd] + 1;
        int down_len_cd = depth[d] - depth[l_cd];
        int len_cd = up_len_cd + down_len_cd;
        
        int min_len = min(len_ab, len_cd);
        if (min_len == 0) {
            cout << "0\n";
            continue;
        }
        
        int low = 0, high = min_len - 1;
        int ans = min_len;
        while (low <= high) {
            int mid = (low + high) / 2;
            int node_ab, node_cd;
            
            // Get character from path a-b at mid
            if (mid < up_len_ab) {
                node_ab = get_kth(a, mid);
            } else {
                int i = mid - up_len_ab;
                int target_depth = depth[l_ab] + 1 + i;
                int steps = depth[b] - target_depth;
                node_ab = get_kth(b, steps);
            }
            
            // Get character from path c-d at mid
            if (mid < up_len_cd) {
                node_cd = get_kth(c, mid);
            } else {
                int i = mid - up_len_cd;
                int target_depth = depth[l_cd] + 1 + i;
                int steps = depth[d] - target_depth;
                node_cd = get_kth(d, steps);
            }
            
            if (node_ab == -1 || node_cd == -1 || s[node_ab-1] != s[node_cd-1]) {
                ans = mid;
                high = mid - 1;
            } else {
                low = mid + 1;
            }
        }
        cout << ans << '\n';
    }
    
    return 0;

Inverters (digital circuits that invert a logic signal, so that a 0 at the input produces a 1 at the output, and vice versa) are essentially voltage amplifiers and can be used to make relaxation oscillators in a number of ways. A circuit related to that of the illustration uses a loop of two inverters and a capacitor C to provide positive feedback, with a resistor R in parallel with one of the inverters to provide an RC charging time to set frequency. This circuit is commonly given as a simple example, but there are a number of problems with using it, such as that the input voltage to the first gate sometimes exceeds the specified limits for practical gates. A more practical digital relaxation oscillator, called a ring oscillator, consists simply of a ring containing an odd number N (greater than 1) of inverters. See Logic circuits

#### Sine-wave oscillators

Oscillators in the second major class have their oscillation frequency set by a linear circuit, and their amplitudes set by a weak nonlinearity.

A simple example of a suitable linear circuit is a two-component loop consisting of an ideal inductor [whose voltage is given by Eq. (3),

(3)
where i is its current] and a capacitor [whose current is given by Eq. (4)],
(4)
connected in parallel. These are said to be linear elements because, in a sense, output is directly proportional to input, for example, doubling the voltage v across a capacitor also doubles dv/dt and therefore doubles i. The overall differential equation for a capacitor-inductor loop can be written as Eq. (5). (5)  Mathematically this has solutions of the form of Eq. (6),
(6)
where &ohgr; = 1/LC [which means that the circuit oscillates at a frequency 1/(2&pgr;LC)] and A and &phgr; are undefined. They are undefined precisely because the elements in the circuit are linear and do not vary with time: any solution (possible behavior) to the equation can be scaled arbitrarily or time-shifted arbitrarily to give another. Practically, A and &phgr; are determined by weak nonlinearities in a circuit.

Equation (5) is a good first approximation to the equation describing a pendulum, and so has a long history as an accurate timekeeper. Its value as an oscillator comes from Galileo's original observation that the frequency of oscillation (&ohgr;/2&pgr;) is independent of the amplitude A. This contrasts sharply with the case of the relaxation oscillator, where any drift in the amplitude (resulting from a threshold shift in a comparator, for instance) can translate directly into a change of frequency. Equation (5) also fundamentally describes the operation of the quartz crystal that has replaced the pendulum as a timekeeper; the physical resonance of the crystal occurs at a time constant defined by its spring constant and its mass.

#### Frequency locking

If an external signal is injected into an oscillator, the natural frequency of oscillation may be affected. If the external signal is periodic, oscillation may lock to the external frequency, a multiple of it, or a submultiple of it, or exhibit an irregular behavior known as chaos.

This locking behavior occurs in all oscillators, sometimes corrupting intended behavior (as when an oscillator locks unintentionally to a harmonic of the power-line frequency) and sometimes by design. An important example of an oscillator that exploits this locking principle is the human heart. Small portions of heart muscle act as relaxation oscillators. They contract, incidentally producing an output voltage that is coupled to their neighbors. For a short time the muscle then recovers from the contraction. As it recovers, it begins to become sensitive to externally applied voltages that can trigger it to contract again (although it will eventually contract anyway). Each small section of heart muscle is thus an independent oscillator, electrically coupled to its neighbors, but the whole heart is synchronized by the frequency-locking mechanism.

## oscillator

i. An electronic circuit that converts energy from a direct-current source to a periodically varying electric output.
ii. The stage of a superheterodyne receiver that generates a radio-frequency signal of the correct frequency to mix with the incoming carrier signal to produce the intermediate-frequency value of the receiver.
iii. The stage of a transmitter that generates the carrier frequency or some fraction of the carrier frequency.

## oscillator

An electronic circuit used to generate high-frequency pulses. See crystal oscillator, VCO and clock.

#include <bits/stdc++.h>
using namespace std;

using ll = long long;
const ll INF = 1e18;

struct Node {
    int children[26];
    int fail;
    int sum_initial;
    ll sum_total;

    Node() {
        memset(children, -1, sizeof children);
        fail = 0;
        sum_initial = 0;
        sum_total = 0;
    }
};

vector<Node> nodes;

vector<vector<ll>> multiply(const vector<vector<ll>>& a, const vector<vector<ll>>& b) {
    int n = a.size();
    vector<vector<ll>> res(n, vector<ll>(n, -INF));
    for (int i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
        for (int k = 0; k < n; ++k) {
            if (a[i][k] == -INF) continue;
            for (int j = 0; j < n; ++j) {
                if (b[k][j] == -INF) continue;
                res[i][j] = max(res[i][j], a[i][k] + b[k][j]);
            }
        }
    }
    return res;
}

vector<vector<ll>> matrix_pow(vector<vector<ll>> a, ll power) {
    int n = a.size();
    vector<vector<ll>> res(n, vector<ll>(n, -INF));
    for (int i = 0; i < n; ++i)
        res[i][i] = 0;

    while (power > 0) {
        if (power % 2 == 1)
            res = multiply(res, a);
        a = multiply(a, a);
        power /= 2;
    }
    return res;
}

int main() {
    ios_base::sync_with_stdio(false);
    cin.tie(0);

    int n; ll l;
    cin >> n >> l;

    vector<int> a(n);
    for (int i = 0; i < n; ++i)
        cin >> a[i];

    nodes.emplace_back();

    for (int i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
        string s;
        cin >> s;
        int current = 0;
        for (char c : s) {
            int idx = c - 'a';
            if (nodes[current].children[idx] == -1) {
                nodes.emplace_back();
                nodes[current].children[idx] = nodes.size() - 1;
            }
            current = nodes[current].children[idx];
        }
        nodes[current].sum_initial += a[i];
    }

    queue<int> q;
    for (int i = 0; i < 26; ++i) {
        if (nodes[0].children[i] != -1) {
            int child = nodes[0].children[i];
            nodes[child].fail = 0;
            q.push(child);
        }
    }

    while (!q.empty()) {
        int u = q.front();
        q.pop();

        for (int c = 0; c < 26; ++c) {
            int v = nodes[u].children[c];
            if (v == -1) continue;

            int f = nodes[u].fail;
            while (f != 0 && nodes[f].children[c] == -1)
                f = nodes[f].fail;

            if (nodes[f].children[c] != -1)
                f = nodes[f].children[c];

            nodes[v].fail = f;
            nodes[v].sum_total = nodes[v].sum_initial + nodes[f].sum_total;
            q.push(v);
        }
    }

    int num_states = nodes.size();
    vector<vector<ll>> M(num_states, vector<ll>(num_states, -INF));

    for (int s = 0; s < num_states; ++s) {
        for (int c = 0; c < 26; ++c) {
            int current = s;
            while (current != 0 && nodes[current].children[c] == -1)
                current = nodes[current].fail;

            if (nodes[current].children[c] != -1)
                current = nodes[current].children[c];

            int t = current;
            ll gain = nodes[t].sum_total;
            if (gain > M[s][t])
                M[s][t] = gain;
        }
    }

    auto M_pow = matrix_pow(M, l);
    ll ans = 0;
    for (int j = 0; j < num_states; ++j)
        ans = max(ans, M_pow[0][j]);

    cout << ans << endl;

    return 0;

So the quartic equation is x⁴ - 2a x² - x + a² - a = 0.

This is a quartic, which can be challenging. Maybe there is a way to factor it. Let's try to factor it as a quadratic in x². Wait, but there's a linear term in x. So maybe not. Alternatively, maybe it factors into two quadratics.

Suppose:

x⁴ - 2a x² - x + a² - a = (x² + p x + q)(x² + r x + s)

Multiply out:

x⁴ + (p + r)x³ + (q + s + pr)x² + (ps + qr)x + q s

Set equal to x⁴ - 2a x² - x + a² - a.

So, matching coefficients:

1. Coefficient of x⁴: 1 = 1, okay.

2. Coefficient of x³: p + r = 0. So, r = -p.

3. Coefficient of x²: q + s + p r = -2a. But since r = -p, this becomes q + s - p² = -2a.

4. Coefficient of x: p s + q r = -1. Again, with r = -p, this becomes p s - q p = -1 => p(s - q) = -1.

5. Constant term: q s = a² - a.

So, we have a system of equations:

1. r = -p

2. q + s - p² = -2a

3. p(s - q) = -1

4. q s = a² - a

Let me see if we can solve this system.

From equation 3: p(s - q) = -1 => s - q = -1/p.

From equation 2: q + s = -2a + p².

Let me call these two:

s - q = -1/p ... (A)

q + s = p² - 2a ... (B)

If I add (A) and (B):

2s = p² - 2a - 1/p => s = [p² - 2a - 1/p]/2

Similarly, subtracting (A) from (B):

2q = p² - 2a + 1/p => q = [p² - 2a + 1/p]/2

Now, from equation 4: q s = a² - a.

So, substituting q and s into this:

[ (p² - 2a + 1/p)/2 ] * [ (p² - 2a - 1/p)/2 ] = a² - a

Multiply the numerators:

[(p² - 2a)^2 - (1/p)^2 ] / 4 = a² - a

Multiply both sides by 4:

(p² - 2a)^2 - (1/p²) = 4(a² - a)

Let me expand (p² - 2a)^2:

= p⁴ - 4a p² + 4a² - 1/p² = 4a² - 4a

Bring all terms to the left:

p⁴ - 4a p² + 4a² - 1/p² - 4a² + 4a = 0

Simplify:

p⁴ - 4a p² - 1/p² + 4a = 0

Hmm, this is a complicated equation in p. Let's see if we can multiply through by p² to eliminate the denominator:

p²*(p⁴) - 4a p²*(p²) - 1 + 4a p² = 0

So:

p⁶ - 4a p⁴ + 4a p² - 1 = 0

This is a sixth-degree equation in p. That seems even worse. Maybe this approach isn't the best. Maybe there's a better substitution or a way to guess a root.

Alternatively, maybe the quartic equation can be factored if we consider that maybe there's a quadratic factor that is solvable. Alternatively, perhaps the quartic has a repeated root or rational roots.

However every fraction can be written as a leading portion followed by a repeating portion.  For example \$1/3\$ starts with \$0.\$ and then just repeats \$01\$ endlessly.  This corresponds to the bar notation taught in primary school.  For example
$$
1/3 = 0.\overline{01}
$$
Where the portion with the bar is the repeating section.
For numbers with a terminating representation, (e.g. \$1/2 = 0.1\$) the repeating section is just \$0\$, since there are endless implicit zeros at the end of a terminating representation.
We will call the minimum1 length of the repeating section the binary period of the fraction.
Your task is to write a program or function which takes a positive integer \$n\$ as input and outputs the binary period of \$1/n\$.
This is code-golf so answers will be scored in bytes with fewer bytes being better.
OEIS A007733

1: We say minimum because if you duplicate a section again it keeps a valid representation. (e.g. \$0.\overline{01} = 0.\overline{0101}\$)

34 Kirchhoff's Laws An electric circuit consists<#sep#>-

#include <iostream>
#include <vector>
#include <deque>
#include <unordered_set>
#include <utility>

using namespace std;

const int MOD = 1000000007;

struct pair_hash {
    template <class T1, class T2>
    size_t operator () (const pair<T1, T2> &p) const {
        auto h1 = hash<T1>{}(p.first);
        auto h2 = hash<T2>{}(p.second);
        return h1 ^ (h2 << 1);
    }
};

using pos = pair<int, int>;

int main() {
    ios_base::sync_with_stdio(false);
    cin.tie(nullptr);

    long long k;
    int n;
    cin >> k >> n;

    unordered_set<pos, pair_hash> forbidden;
    for (int i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
        int x, y;
        cin >> x >> y;
        forbidden.insert({x, y});
    }

    vector<long long> deltas = {1};
    vector<long long> sums = {1};
    deque<pos> q;
    unordered_set<pos, pair_hash> visited;

    q.push_back({0, 0});
    visited.insert({0, 0});

    bool stabilized = false;
    long long c = -1;
    int step = 0;

    while (!q.empty() && !stabilized && step <= 400) {
        unordered_set<pos, pair_hash> next_q_set;
        int current_size = q.size();

        for (int i = 0; i < current_size; ++i) {
            pos current = q.front();
            q.pop_front();

            const int dx[] = {2, 2, 1, 1, -1, -1, -2, -2};
            const int dy[] = {1, -1, 2, -2, 2, -2, 1, -1};

            for (int j = 0; j < 8; ++j) {
                int x = current.first + dx[j];
                int y = current.second + dy[j];
                pos new_pos = {x, y};

                if (forbidden.count(new_pos)) continue;
                if (!visited.count(new_pos)) {
                    visited.insert(new_pos);
                    next_q_set.insert(new_pos);
                }
            }
        }

        long long current_delta = next_q_set.size();

        if (deltas.size() >= 1) {
            long long prev_delta = deltas.back();
            if (current_delta == prev_delta) {
                stabilized = true;
                c = current_delta;
            }
        }

        deltas.push_back(current_delta);
        sums.push_back((sums.back() + current_delta) % MOD);

        q.clear();
        for (const auto& p : next_q_set) {
            q.push_back(p);
        }

        step++;
    }

    if (k < sums.size()) {
        cout << sums[k] % MOD << endl;
    } else {
        if (stabilized) {
            long long base = sums.back();
            long long steps_remaining = k - (sums.size() - 1);
            steps_remaining %= MOD;
            long long add = (steps_remaining % MOD) * (c % MOD) % MOD;
            long long ans = (base + add) % MOD;
            cout << ans << endl;
        } else {
            cout << sums.back() % MOD << endl;
        }
    }

    return 0;

36 Kirchhoff s Current Law (KCL) Kirchhoff s current law states that the algebraic sum of the currents entering any node is identically zero for all instants of time The phrase algebraic sum indicates that we must take reference directions into account as we add up the currents of elements connected to a particular node one way to take reference directions into account is to use a plus sign when the current is directed d away from a node and a minus sign when the current is directed toward a node, or currents in equal to currents out

38 Kirchhoff s Voltage Law (KVL) Kirchhoff s voltage law states that the algebraic sum of the voltages around any loop in a circuit is identically zero for all time A loop is a closed path through a circuit that does not encounter any intermediate node more than once How many loops does this circuit contain?

39 Kirchhoff s Voltage Law (KVL) The phrase algebraic sum indicates that we must take polarity into account as we add up the voltages of elements that comprise a loop one way to do this is to write the voltage with a plus sign when we encounter the + of the polarity before the and a minus sign when we encounter the of the polarity before the + Write the KVL eqn. associated with the loop containing elements 3 6 of the circuit:

44 Dependent Sources Dependent sources model the situation in which the voltage or current of one circuit element is proportional to the voltage or current of the second circuit element used to model electronic devices such as transistors or amplifiers, e.g., the output voltage of an amplifier is proportional to the voltage input of that amplifier Each hdependent d source consists of ftwo parts: the controlling part and the controlled part the controlling part is either an open circuit or a short circuit the controlled part is either a voltage source or a current source There are four types of dependent source that correspond to the four ways of choosing a controlling part and a controlled part, called: voltage controlled voltage sources (VCVS) current controlled voltage controlled voltage sources (VCVS), current controlled voltage sources (CCVS), voltage controlled current sources (VCCS), and current controlled current sources (CCCS)

A circuit is defined to be a collection<#sep#>-

#include <bits/stdc++.h>
using namespace std;

const int MOD = 1e9 + 7;
int comb[51][51];

void precompute_comb() {
    for (int n = 0; n <= 50; ++n) {
        comb[n][0] = 1;
        comb[n][n] = 1;
        for (int k = 1; k < n; ++k) {
            comb[n][k] = (comb[n - 1][k - 1] + comb[n - 1][k]) % MOD;
        }
    }
}

int main() {
    precompute_comb();
    int n, k;
    cin >> n >> k;
    vector<int> weights(n);
    int a = 0, b = 0;
    for (int& w : weights) {
        cin >> w;
        if (w == 50) ++a;
        else ++b;
    }

    if ((a > 0 && 50 > k) || (b > 0 && 100 > k)) {
        cout << "-1\n0\n";
        return 0;
    }

    int distance[51][51][2];
    long long count[51][51][2];
    memset(distance, -1, sizeof(distance));
    memset(count, 0, sizeof(count));

    queue<tuple<int, int, int>> q;
    distance[a][b][0] = 0;
    count[a][b][0] = 1;
    q.emplace(a, b, 0);

    while (!q.empty()) {
        auto [x_curr, y_curr, pos] = q.front();
        q.pop();
        int curr_dist = distance[x_curr][y_curr][pos];
        long long curr_cnt = count[x_curr][y_curr][pos];

        if (pos == 0) {
            for (int x = 0; x <= x_curr; ++x) {
                for (int y = 0; y <= y_curr; ++y) {
                    if (x + y == 0 || 50 * x + 100 * y > k) continue;
                    int nx = x_curr - x, ny = y_curr - y;
                    if (nx < 0 || ny < 0) continue;
                    int new_pos = 1;
                    long long ways = (comb[x_curr][x] * comb[y_curr][y]) % MOD;
                    if (distance[nx][ny][new_pos] == -1) {
                        distance[nx][ny][new_pos] = curr_dist + 1;
                        count[nx][ny][new_pos] = (curr_cnt * ways) % MOD;
                        q.emplace(nx, ny, new_pos);
                    } else if (distance[nx][ny][new_pos] == curr_dist + 1) {
                        count[nx][ny][new_pos] = (count[nx][ny][new_pos] + curr_cnt * ways) % MOD;
                    }
                }
            }
        } else {
            int right_x = a - x_curr;
            int right_y = b - y_curr;
            for (int x = 0; x <= right_x; ++x) {
                for (int y = 0; y <= right_y; ++y) {
                    if (x + y == 0 || 50 * x + 100 * y > k) continue;
                    int nx = x_curr + x, ny = y_curr + y;
                    int new_pos = 0;
                    long long ways = (comb[right_x][x] * comb[right_y][y]) % MOD;
                    if (distance[nx][ny][new_pos] == -1) {
                        distance[nx][ny][new_pos] = curr_dist + 1;
                        count[nx][ny][new_pos] = (curr_cnt * ways) % MOD;
                        q.emplace(nx, ny, new_pos);
                    } else if (distance[nx][ny][new_pos] == curr_dist + 1) {
                        count[nx][ny][new_pos] = (count[nx][ny][new_pos] + curr_cnt * ways) % MOD;
                    }
                }
            }
        }
    }

    int res_dist = distance[0][0][1];
    long long res_cnt = res_dist == -1 ? 0 : count[0][0][1] % MOD;
    cout << res_dist << '\n' << res_cnt << '\n';

    return 0;
}<#sep#> a

Physics 7B-1 (A/B) Professor Cebra Winter 2010 Lecture 2 Simple Circuits Slide 1<#sep#>-

#include <vector>
#include <unordered_set>
#include <algorithm>
#include <iostream>
#include <functional>

using namespace std;

struct Constraint {
    vector<int> vars;
};

int main() {
    int n, k;
    cin >> n >> k;

    vector<Constraint> constraints;
    int s = 0;

    for (int i = 0; i < k; ++i) {
        int m;
        cin >> m;
        Constraint c;
        for (int j = 0; j < m; ++j) {
            int var;
            cin >> var;
            var--; // convert to 0-based
            c.vars.push_back(var);
        }
        constraints.push_back(c);
        if (m > s) {
            s = m;
        }
    }

    vector<Constraint> s_constraints;
    for (const auto& c : constraints) {
        if (c.vars.size() == s) {
            s_constraints.push_back(c);
        }
    }

    vector<vector<int>> var_to_s_constraints(n);
    for (int i = 0; i < s_constraints.size(); ++i) {
        for (int var : s_constraints[i].vars) {
            var_to_s_constraints[var].push_back(i);
        }
    }

    vector<unordered_set<int>> available(s_constraints.size());
    for (int i = 0; i < s_constraints.size(); ++i) {
        for (int v = 1; v <= s; ++v) {
            available[i].insert(v);
        }
    }

    vector<int> assignment(n, 0);

    function<bool(int)> backtrack = [&](int var) {
        if (var == n) {
            for (const auto& c : constraints) {
                if (c.vars.size() == s) continue;
                unordered_set<int> seen;
                for (int v : c.vars) {
                    if (seen.count(assignment[v])) return false;
                    seen.insert(assignment[v]);
                }
            }
            return true;
        }

        for (int v = 1; v <= s; ++v) {
            bool valid = true;

            for (int idx : var_to_s_constraints[var]) {
                if (available[idx].count(v) == 0) {
                    valid = false;
                    break;
                }
                int remaining_vars = 0;
                for (int x : s_constraints[idx].vars) {
                    if (x == var) continue;
                    if (assignment[x] == 0) remaining_vars++;
                }
                if (remaining_vars != (available[idx].size() - 1)) {
                    valid = false;
                    break;
                }
            }

            if (!valid) continue;

            for (const auto& c : constraints) {
                if (c.vars.size() == s) continue;
                bool contains_var = false;
                for (int x : c.vars) {
                    if (x == var) {
                        contains_var = true;
                        break;
                    }
                }
                if (!contains_var) continue;

                for (int other_var : c.vars) {
                    if (other_var == var) continue;
                    if (assignment[other_var] == v) {
                        valid = false;
                        break;
                    }
                }
                if (!valid) break;
            }

            if (!valid) continue;

            assignment[var] = v;
            vector<pair<int, int>> removed;

            for (int idx : var_to_s_constraints[var]) {
                if (available[idx].erase(v)) {
                    removed.emplace_back(idx, v);
                }
            }

            if (backtrack(var + 1)) {
                return true;
            }

            assignment[var] = 0;
            for (const auto& [idx, val] : removed) {
                available[idx].insert(val);
            }
        }

        return false;
    };

    if (backtrack(0)) {
        for (int x : assignment) {
            cout << x << " ";
        }
        cout << endl;
    }

    return 0;
}<#sep#> the same quartic as before but in terms of y. So this substitution didn't help.

Alternatively, perhaps assuming a solution in terms of sqrt(a). Let me try x = k sqrt(a), where k is a constant to be determined. Substitute into the original equation:

k sqrt(a) = sqrt(a - sqrt(a +k sqrt(a))).

Square both sides:

k² a =a - sqrt(a +k sqrt(a))

Then, sqrt(a +k sqrt(a))=a -k² a.

Square again:

a +k sqrt(a)=a² -2a(k² a) + (k² a)^2.

Wait, simplifying:

Left side: a +k sqrt(a)

Right side: a² -2a*(k² a) + (k² a)^2 = a² -2a² k² +k⁴ a².

Therefore, equation becomes:

a +k sqrt(a) = a²(1 -2k² +k⁴).

Divide both sides by a (since a>0):

1 + (k / sqrt(a)) = a(1 -2k² +k⁴).

But this seems messy. Not sure if this helps.

Alternatively, if we suppose that k is a constant independent of a. For example, when a=2, k= (sqrt(5)-1)/2 / sqrt(2). Which is not a nice constant, so maybe this approach is not fruitful.

Alternatively, let's try to find a substitution z = x + sqrt(a +x). Wait, but maybe not.

Alternatively, maybe let u = sqrt(a +x). Then from original equation:

x = sqrt(a - u)

Then, u = sqrt(a +x) = sqrt(a + sqrt(a - u))

Square both sides:

u² = a + sqrt(a - u)

Hmm, similar to before. Then this becomes a recursive equation, but maybe we can square again.

Let me write:

u² -a = sqrt(a - u)

Square both sides:

(u² -a)^2 = a -u

Expand left side:

u⁴ -2a u² +a² = a -u

Bring all terms to left:

u⁴ -2a u² +a² -a +u =0

Which is the same quartic as before in terms of u. So not helpful.

This seems to go in circles. Maybe instead, let's consider that the original equation can be transformed into a quadratic equation through some substitution.

Let me recall that when we set t = x² -a, the equation became t² = x +a. But t = x² -a ⇒ x² = t +a. So:

t² =x +a

x² = t +a

Let me attempt to solve this system:

From x² = t +a and t² =x +a.

So substitute x = t² -a into x² = t +a:

(t² -a)^2 = t +a

Expand left side:

t⁴ -2a t² +a² = t +a

Which is the same quartic equation: t⁴ -2a t² -t +a² -a =0.

No progress.

Alternatively, maybe subtract the two equations:

x² - t² = (t +a) - (x +a) ⇒ (x -t)(x +t) = t -x ⇒ (x -t)(x +t +1)=0.

Therefore, either x = t or x + t +1=0.

But x and t are related by x² = t +a and t² =x +a.

Case 1: x = t.

If x = t, then substituting into x² = x +a:

x² -x -a=0

Solutions are x=(1 ± sqrt(1 +4a))/2.

But since x ≥0, we take x=(1 + sqrt(1 +4a))/2.

Now, we need to check if this solution satisfies the original equation.

Let me verify.

If x=(1 + sqrt(1 +4a))/2, then check if x = sqrt(a - sqrt(a +x)).

Compute the right-hand side:

sqrt(a - sqrt(a +x)).

First, compute a +x =a + [1 + sqrt(1 +4a)]/2 = [2a +1 + sqrt(1 +4a)]/2.

Then, sqrt(a +x) = sqrt([2a +1 + sqrt(1 +4a)]/2).

Then, a - sqrt(a +x) = a - sqrt([2a +1 + sqrt(1 +4a)]/2).

Then, sqrt(a - sqrt(a +x)) should equal x.

But let's compute for a=2. If a=2, x=(1 + sqrt(9))/2=(1+3)/2=2. But earlier we saw that x=2 does not satisfy the original equation for a=2. So this solution is extraneous.

Wait, but when a=2, x=(1 + sqrt(9))/2=2, which was not a solution. So this suggests that even though we get x=(1 + sqrt(1 +4a))/2 from Case 1, it might not satisfy the original equation. Therefore, this is an extraneous solution introduced during squaring. Thus, Case 1 gives extraneous solutions.

Case 2: x + t +1=0.

Since x and t are related by x² = t +a and t² =x +a, let's substitute t = -x -1 into x² = t +a:

x² = (-x -1) +a ⇒x² +x +1 -a=0.

Quadratic equation: x² +x + (1 -a)=0.

Solutions are x=(-1 ± sqrt(1 -4(1 -a)))/2 = (-1 ± sqrt(4a -3))/2.

Now, since x must be ≥0, the solutions are valid only if sqrt(4a -3) is real and the numerator is non-negative.

sqrt(4a -3) requires 4a -3 ≥0 ⇒a ≥3/4.

For the solutions to be non-negative:

x = [ -1 + sqrt(4a -3) ] / 2.

Since sqrt(4a -3) can be greater than or equal to 1 when a ≥1, which would make x ≥0.

But also, we must ensure that this solution satisfies the original equation.

Let me check for a=2. Then, x=( -1 + sqrt(5) )/2 ≈ (-1 +2.236)/2≈0.618, which works as we saw earlier. For a=1, 4a -3=1, so x=( -1 +1)/2=0, which works since when a=1, x=0 is a solution.

Another example: a=3/4. Then 4a -3=0, so x=(-1 +0)/2= -0.5, which is negative, so invalid. So for a=3/4, this gives x=-0.5, which is invalid. Therefore, valid solutions only exist when 4a -3 ≥0 and x ≥0. Which requires:

sqrt(4a -3) -1 ≥0 ⇒ sqrt(4a -3) ≥1 ⇒4a -3≥1 ⇒4a≥4 ⇒a≥1.

So, for a≥1, x=( -1 + sqrt(4a -3) ) /2 is non-negative. Let me confirm:

For a=1, sqrt(4*1 -3)=sqrt(1)=1 ⇒x=( -1 +1)/2=0.

For a=2, sqrt(5)-1)/2≈0.618.

For a=5, sqrt(17)-1)/2≈(4.123-1)/2≈1.5615. Check if this is a solution.

x≈1.5615. Original equation: x= sqrt(a - sqrt(a +x)).

Compute a +x=5 +1.5615≈6.5615. sqrt≈2.5615. Then a - sqrt(a+x)=5 -2.5615≈2.4385. sqrt(2.4385)≈1.5615=x. So it works.

Therefore, the Case 2 solution x=(sqrt(4a -3) -1)/2 works for a≥1. Also, check if for a=1, this gives x=0, which is the correct solution.

Therefore, putting this together:

The only real solutions are x=(sqrt(4a -3) -1)/2 for a≥1, and no solutions for a<1.

But let me verify for a=1. When a=1, x=(sqrt(4*1 -3)-1)/2=(sqrt(
